Non-wellfounded parsimonious proofs and non-uniform complexity
Matteo Acclavio, Gianluca Curzi, Giulio Guerrieri

Abstract
. In this paper we investigate the complexity-theoretical aspects of cyclic and non-wellfounded proofs in the context of parsimonious logic, a variant of linear logic where the exponential modality ! is interpreted as a constructor for streams over finite data. We present non-wellfounded parsimonious proof systems capturing the classes 𝐅𝐏 \mathbf{FP} and 𝐅𝐏 / 𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒 \mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly} . Soundness is established via a polynomial modulus of continuity for continuous cut-elimination. Completeness relies on an encoding of polynomial Turing machines with advice within a type assignment system based on parsimonious logic. As a byproduct of our proof methods, we establish a series of characterisation results for various finitary proof systems.



1.
Introduction

In its modern guise,
non-wellfounded proof theory
emerged for the first time in the context of the modal
\mu
-calculus
[
NW96
,
DHL06
]
.
Since then, this area of proof theory has provided a promising theoretical framework for studying least and greatest fixed points, hence for reasoning about induction and coinduction. What is more, its applications have spanned, over the years, a number of rather diverse topics, such as predicate logic
[
BS11
,
BT19
]
, algebras
[
DP17
,
DP18
,
DD24a
,
DD24b
]
, arithmetic
[
Sim17
,
BT17
,
Das20b
]
, proofs-as-programs interpretations
[
BDS16
,
DS19
,
Das20a
,
KPP21
,
Das21
]
, modal logics and
\mu
-calculi
[
NW96
,
DHL06
,
SD03a
,
SD03b
,
DKMV23
,
AMP24
,
BGH+25
,
KDV25
]
, and continuous cut elimination
[
Min78
,
FS13
,
Sau23
]
.

Non-wellfounded proof theory studies proofs whose underlying tree structure is possibly infinite (but finitely branching), where logical consistency is guaranteed by appropriate global proof-theoretic conditions, called
progressing

criteria
. Within this framework, the so-called
regular
proofs represent a major focus of interest. These are special non-wellfounded proofs having only finitely many distinct subproofs, and admit a finite description in terms of cyclic directed graphs. Because of their graph-theoretic representation, these proofs are also called
circular
or
cyclic
.

In
[
Das20a
,
KPP21
,
Das21
]
non-wellfounded proof-theory has been investigated from the perspective of the
Curry-Howard correspondence
, where proofs are interpreted as (functional) programs, and program execution is given in terms of cut elimination. Non-wellfounded proofs can be understood as programs defined by a possibly infinite list of instructions, where the progressing criterion ensures
totality
, i.e., that those programs are always well-defined on all arguments.
On the other hand, the regularity condition on proof trees has a natural counterpart in the notion of
computational uniformity
, meaning that programs defined by regular proofs can always be described by a
finite
sets of machine instructions.

In
[
CD22
]
the second author and Das extended the computational reading of non-wellfounded proofs to the realm of
computational complexity
, introducing circular proof systems capturing the class of functions computable in polynomial time (
\mathbf{FP}
) and the elementary functions (
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{ELEMENTARY}
). These proof systems are based on Bellantoni and Cook’s algebra of functions for safe recursion
[
BC92
]
, and are defined by identifying global conditions on circular progressing proofs motivated by ideas from
Implicit Computational Complexity
(ICC), which studies machine-free characterisations of complexity classes that do not rely on explicit bounds on resources. This paper substantially launched a new topic in ICC called
Cyclic Implicit Complexity
(CIC).

The results in
[
CD22
]
have been generalized by the same authors in
[
CD23
]

to capture the class of functions computable in polynomial time by Turing machines with access to
polynomial advice
(
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
) or, equivalently, computable by non-uniform families of polynomial-size circuits (see, e.g.,
[
AB09
]
).
Specifically,
non-uniform complexity
is modeled by more permissive non-wellfounded proof systems (compared to circular proof systems), obtained by weakening the regularity condition, hence relaxing finite presentability of proofs
1
1

1

Note that
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
includes
undecidable problems
, and so cannot be characterised by purely circular proof systems, which typically represent only computable functions.
.

In this paper, we take an alternative route to CIC based on
linear logic

[
Gir94
]
.
Linear logic (
\mathsf{LL}
) is a refinement of both classical and intuitionistic logic that allows a better control over computational resources thanks to the so-called
exponential modalities
(denoted by
\oc
and
\wn
), which mark the distinction between those assumptions that can be used linearly (that is, exactly once), and the ones that are reusable at will. According to the Curry-Howard reading of linear logic, these modalities introduce non-linearity in functional programs: a proof of the linear implication
\oc A\multimap B
is interpreted as a program returning an output of type
B
using an arbitrary number of times (including zero times) an input of type
A
.

Linear logic has inspired a variety of methods for taming complexity. The central idea is to weaken the exponential rules for inducing a bound on cut elimination, which reduces the computational strength of the system. These restricted systems of linear logic are called “light logics”. Examples are
soft linear logic

[
Laf04
]
or
light linear logic

[
Gir94
]
for
\mathbf{FP}
, and
elementary linear logic

[
DJ03
,
Bai15
]
for
\mathbf{F}\mathbf{ELEMENTARY}
.
Light logics are typically endowed with second-order quantifiers, which allow for a direct encoding of (resource-bounded) Turing machines, the crucial step for proving completeness w.r.t. a complexity class.

Continuing this tradition, in a series of papers
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
Mazza introduced
parsimonious logic
(
\mathsf{PL}
), a
type system based on a
variant of linear logic (defined in a type-theoretic fashion) where the exponential modality
\oc
satisfies Milner’s law (i.e.,
\oc A\multimapboth A\otimes\oc A
) and invalidates the implications
\oc A\multimap\oc\oc A
(
digging
) and
\oc A\multimap\oc A\otimes\oc A
(
contraction
).
In parsimonious logic, a proof of
\oc A
can be interpreted as a
stream
over proofs of
A
, i.e., as a greatest fixed point.
The linear implications
A\otimes\oc A\multimap\oc A
(
co-absorption
) and
\oc A\multimap A\otimes\oc A
(
absorption
), which form the two directions of Milner’s law, can be read computationally as the
push
and
pop
operations on streams.
In particular, in
[
MT15
]
Mazza and Terui presented
non-uniform parsimonious logic
(
\mathsf{nuPL}
), an extension of
\mathsf{PL}
equipped with an infinitely branching rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
(see
Figure

3
) that constructs a stream
({\mathcal{D}_{f(0)}},{\mathcal{D}_{f(1)}},\ldots,\allowbreak{\mathcal{D}_{f(n)}},\ldots)
of type
\oc A
from a finite set of proofs
\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n}
of
A
and a (possibly non-recursive) function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

The fundamental result of
[
MT15
]
is that, when endowed with restricted second-order quantifiers, the logic
\mathsf{PL}
(resp.
\mathsf{nuPL}
) characterises the class
\mathbf{P}
of problems decidable in polynomial time (resp. the class
\mathbf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
of problems decidable by polynomial size families of circuits)
2
2

2

Despite not explicitly stated in
[
MT15
]
, the characterisation of
\mathbf{P}
is a direct byproduct.

.
On the one hand, the infinitely branching rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
can be used to encode streams, hence to model Turing machines querying an
advice

[
AB09
]
; on the other hand, the absence of digging and contraction induces a polynomial bound on normalisation.

The analysis of parsimonious logic conducted in
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
reveals that fixed point-based definitions of the exponentials are better behaving when digging and contraction are discarded. However, these results rely on the co-absorption rule (
\oc\mathsf{b}
in
Figure

3
) which is not admissible in
\mathsf{LL}
.
Proper subsystems of
\mathsf{LL}
(free of the co-absorption rule) admitting a stream-based interpretation of the exponentials have been provided in our previous work
[
ACG24
]
, where we have defined
parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{PLL}
) and its non-uniform version, called
non-uniform parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{nuPLL}
).
Furthermore, in this work we also recast
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}
in a non-wellfounded framework
\mathsf{PLL}^{\infty}
by identifying appropriate global conditions that duly reflect the proof-theoretic features of these systems.
As a result, we introduced
regular parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{rPLL}^{\infty}
), defined in terms of regular non-wellfounded proofs, and
weakly regular parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{wrPLL}^{\infty}
), where regularity is relaxed to model non-uniform computation.
The main contribution of
[
ACG24
]
is a
continuous cut elimination theorem
for
\mathsf{rPLL}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{wrPLL}^{\infty}
, obtained by applying a novel cut elimination technique in the
non-wellfounded setting
.

Contributions

In this paper, we define second-order extensions (noted with the subscript
2\ell
) of the proof systems presented in
[
ACG24
]
, and we establish the characterisations below:

•

\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
) characterise
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
;

•

\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(and
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
) characterise
\mathbf{FP}
.

The interconnections between our results are summarised in
Figure

1
.
The key result for this characterisations is the polynomial modulus of continuity on cut elimination for
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, (
Lemma

5.25
),
from which we infer that
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is
sound
for
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
, and that
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is sound for
\mathbf{FP}
(
Theorem

5.27
.
1
-
2
).

Completeness
(
Theorem

6.38
requires a series of intermediate steps.
We first introduce the type system
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
, which implements a form of stream-based computation. This system represents an alternative approach to the type systems for parsimonious logic introduced by Mazza et al. in
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
.
Then, we describe an encoding of polynomial time Turing machines with (polynomial) advice within
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
, which allows us to prove that
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
is complete for
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
(
Theorem

6.29
.
2
).
This is done by adapting standard methods from
[
MT03
,
GRDR09
]
to the setting of non-uniform computation. Thirdly, we define a translation from
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
to
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
(
Theorem

6.36
.
2
).
Finally, we show that computation over strings in
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
can be simulated within
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(
Theorem

4.39
.
2
).
A similar completeness argument can be restated for
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(and
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
) by considering the type system
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}

(
Theorem

6.29
.
1
,
Theorem

6.36
.
1
, and
Theorem

4.39
.
1
).

On a technical side, the present paper contributes to the previous literature on the topic in several ways:

•

Cyclic Implicit Complexity (CIC).

The contribution of this paper advances the development of CIC and explores it for the first time in the setting of linear logic.
Compared to previous work on CIC, this paper adopts rather different techniques for achieving soundness and completeness results.
In particular, the proof of soundness introduces novel ideas to estimate moduli of continuity.

•

Parsimonious logic.
This paper lifts to a non-wellfounded proof-theoretic setting the characterisation results for parsimonious logic presented by Mazza at al. in
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
, improving certain aspects of the latter.
First, as already anticipated, our characterisation theorems do not require the presence of the
co-absorption rule
, which is expressed by the formula
A\otimes\oc A\multimap\oc A
. Computationally, this means that it is possible to encode polytime Turing machines (with polynomial advice) in our systems
without
the need of a “push” operation on streams. Secondly, we generalise the characterisation of classes of problems given in
[
MT15
]
(i.e.,
\mathbf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
and
\mathbf{P}
) to classes of functions (i.e.,
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
and
\mathbf{FP}
). Last, non-wellfounded proofs do not rely on infinitely branching rules such as
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
, avoiding the introduction of the constant growth-rate function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to\{1,\ldots,n\}
and making our characterisations more “implicit”, thus closer to ICC.

Outline of the paper

introduces some preliminary notions and results on linear logic, non-wellfounded proofs and (non-uniform) complexity classes.

Sections

and
4
recall parsimonious linear logic and extend to a second-order setting the proof systems introduced in
[
ACG24
]
. Specifically, in
Section

we define the proof system
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
. In
Section

we consider their non-wellfounded proof-theoretic versions
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, and study cut elimination properties and simulation results relating the systems.

Sections

5
and
6
represent the main contribution of this paper. In
Section

5
we prove the soundness theorem, and in
Section

6
we define the type systems
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
based on parsimonious logic and use them to establish completeness.

2.
Preliminary notions

In this section we recall some basic notions and notation from (non-wellfounded) proof theory and computational complexity.

2.1.
Derivations and coderivations

We assume that the reader is familiar with the syntax of sequent calculus, e.g.
[
TS00
]
.
Here we specify some conventions adopted to simplify the content of this paper.

We consider (
sequent
)
rules
of the form

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

or

\Gamma_{1}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

or

\Gamma_{1}

\Gamma_{2}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

\Gamma

,
and we refer
to the sequents
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
as the
premises
,
and
to the sequent
\Gamma
as the
conclusion
of the rule
\mathsf{r}
.
Following
[
BDS16
]
, we define
sequents
as finite sets of pairwise disjoint formula occurrences
3
3

3

This can be done by associating with any formula occurrence an address, as done in
[
BDS16
]
. The benefit of this sequent calculus presentation is that we can track formula occurrences along a branch of the prooftree while avoiding some technicalities involved in the sequents-as-lists presentation.
. In particular, when we refer to a formula in a sequent we always consider a
specific occurrence
of it.

Definition 2.1
.

A (binary, possibly infinite)
tree

\mathcal{T}
is a subset of words in
\{1,2\}^{*}

that contains the empty word
\epsilon
(the
root
of
\mathcal{T}
)
and is
ordered-prefix-closed

(i.e., if
n\in\{1,2\}
and
vn\in\mathcal{T}
, then
v\in\mathcal{T}
,
and
if moreover
v2\in\mathcal{T}
, then
v1\in\mathcal{T}
).
The elements of
\mathcal{T}
are called
nodes
and their
height
is the length of the word.
A
child
of
v\in\mathcal{T}
is any
vn\in\mathcal{T}
with
n\in\{1,2\}
.
The
prefix order
is a partial order
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
on
\mathcal{T}
defined by: for any
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
,
v\leq_{\mathcal{T}}v^{\prime}
if
v^{\prime}=vw
for some
w\in\{1,2\}^{*}
.
A maximal element of
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
is a
leaf
of
\mathcal{T}
.
A
branch
of
\mathcal{T}
is a set
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
such that
\epsilon\in\mathcal{B}
and if
w\in\mathcal{B}
is not a leaf of
\mathcal{T}
then
w
has exactly one child in
\mathcal{B}
.

A
coderivation
over a set of rules
\mathcal{S}

is a labeling
\mathcal{D}
of a tree
\mathcal{T}
by sequents such that
if
v
is a node of
\mathcal{T}
with children
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
(with
n\in\{0,1,2\}
),
then there is an occurrence of a rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{S}
with conclusion the sequent
\mathcal{D}(v)
and premises the sequents
\mathcal{D}(v_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{D}(v_{n})
.
The
height
of
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{D}
is the height of
v\in\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{D}(v)
is the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}
.
The
conclusion
of
\mathcal{D}
is the sequent
\mathcal{D}(\epsilon)
.
If
v
is a node of the tree, the
sub-coderivation
of
\mathcal{D}
rooted at
v
is the coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{v}
defined by
\mathcal{D}_{v}(w)=\mathcal{D}(vw)
.
It is
regular
if it has finitely many distinct sub-coderivations; it is
non-wellfounded
if it labels an infinite tree, and
it is a
derivation
(with
size

|\mathcal{D}|\in\mathbb{N}
) if it labels a finite tree (with
|\mathcal{D}|
nodes).

Regular coderivations (aka circular or cyclic) can be represented as
finite
directed (possibly cyclic) graphs: a cycle is created by linking the roots of two identical subcoderivations.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a coderivation labeling a tree
\mathcal{T}
.
A
bar
(resp.
prebar
) of
\mathcal{D}
is a set
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
where:

•

any branch (resp. infinite branch) of the tree
\mathcal{T}
underlying
\mathcal{D}
contains a node in
\mathcal{V}
;

•

any pair of nodes in
\mathcal{V}
are mutually incomparable with respect to the prefix order
\leq_{\mathcal{T}}
.

2.2.
(Non-uniform) complexity classes

We recall that
\mathbf{FP}
is the class of functions computable in polynomial time by a Turing machine, and that
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
is the class of functions computable in polynomial time by a Turing machine with access to a “polynomial amount of advice” (determined only by the length of the input). Formally:

Definition 2.3
.

\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
is the class of functions

f(\vec{x})
for which, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a string (called the
advice
)
\alpha_{n}
of length polynomial in
n
and
f^{\prime}(y,\vec{x})\in\mathbf{FP}
such that

f(\vec{x})=f^{\prime}(\alpha_{|\vec{x}|},\vec{x})
.

\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
extends
\mathbf{FP}
and contains some incomputable functions, for instance the characteristic function of undecidable unary languages
[
AB09
, Example 6.4]
.
The class
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
can be also defined in terms of non-uniform families of circuits.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
AB09
]
, Thm. 6.11)
.

A function
f
is in
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
iff there is polynomial-size familiy of circuits computing
f
.

Following
[
CD23
]
, we adopt a different presentation of
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
that eases the proof of completeness.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}=\{r\colon\mathbb{N}\to\{0,1\}\}
and
\mathbf{FP}(\mathbb{R})
be the set of functions computable in polynomial time by a Turing machine with access to an oracle
r\in\mathbb{R}
.

4
4

4

Note that the elements of
\mathbb{R}
may be identified with Boolean streams.

Proposition 2.6

(See, e.g.,
[
CD23
]
)
.

\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}=\mathbf{FP}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof 2.7

(Proof sketch)
.

For the left-right inclusion, let
p(n)
be a polynomial and
\mathbf{C}=(C_{n})_{n<\omega}
be a circuit family with each
C_{n}
taking
n
Boolean inputs and having size
<p(n)
. We need to show that the language computed by
\mathbf{C}
is also computed in
\mathbf{FP}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
c\in\mathbb{R}
be the function that, on inputs
x,y
returns the
|y|
th
bit of
C_{|x|}
.
Using this oracle we can compute
C_{|x|}
by polynomially queries to
c
, and this may be evaluated as usual using a polynomial-time evaluator in
\mathbf{FP}
. For the right-left inclusion, notice that a polynomial-time machine can only make polynomially many calls to oracles with inputs of only polynomial size. Thus, if
f\in\mathbf{FP}(\mathbb{R})
then there is some
p_{f}
with
f\in\mathbf{FP}(\mathbb{R}^{<p_{f}})
, where
\mathbb{R}^{<p_{f}}
is the restriction of each
r\in\mathbb{R}
to only its first
p_{f}(|\vec{x}|)
many bits.
Now, since
f
can only call a fixed number of oracles from
\mathbb{R}
, we can collect these finitely many polynomial-length prefixes into a single advice string for computation in
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
.

3.
Second-Order Parsimonious Linear Logic

In
[
ACG23
]
, we introduced
parsimonious linear logic
(
\mathsf{PLL}
), a subsystem of linear logic inspired by parsimonious logic
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
. In
\mathsf{PLL}
the usual promotion rule is replaced by
functorial promotion

\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
, and the usual contraction and dereliction rules by the
absorption
rule
\wn\mathsf{b}
(see
Figure

3
).
As a consequence, the exponential modalities of
\mathsf{PLL}
are weaker than the usual linear logical modalities. In particular, the
digging
formula
\oc A\multimap\oc\oc A

and the
contraction
formula
\oc A\multimap\oc A\otimes\oc A

are not provable in
\mathsf{PLL}
.

As already remarked in
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
, systems based on parsimonious logic like
\mathsf{PLL}
admit a straightforward computational reading based on streams: on the one hand, functorial promotion can be interpreted as a constructor
\oc(\_):A\multimap\oc A
, which takes a program
M
of type
A
and returns the stream
\oc(M)\coloneqq(M,M,\ldots,M,\ldots)
; on the other hand, the absorption rule can be seen as a
pop
operation on streams
\mathsf{pop}:\oc A\multimap A\otimes\oc A
, which extracts the first element off the stream, i.e.,

\mathsf{pop}(\oc(M))=M\otimes\oc(M)
. This step of computation is reflected by the cut elimination rule governing the interaction between
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
(see
Figure

6
).

The computational interpretation outlined above can be pushed further by considering
non-uniform parsimonious linear logic

\mathsf{nuPLL}
, which replaces
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
with its infinitely branching version
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
in
Figure

3
, called
non-uniform promotion

[
ACG23
,
MT15
,
Maz15
]
,
which allows the construction of general streams of the form
(M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{n},\ldots)
, where the side condition ensures that streams are over finitely many data.

Motivated by our complexity-theoretic goals, we present
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
, second-order versions of
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}
where instantiation of the existential quantifier
\exists
is restricted to
linear formulas
, i.e., (
\oc,\wn
)-free formulas. On the one hand, second-order quantifiers are essential to implement iteration of a polynomial time Turing machine transition function (whose advice will be encoded as a stream via the rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
). On the other hand, the weaker exponential rules of (non-uniform) parsimonious linear logic and the linearity restriction on second-order instantiation together will guarantee a polynomial bound on cut elimination
5
5

5

If we dropped the linearity restriction on
\exists
, cut elimination for
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
would become superexponential. See
Remark

6.41
for an example formulated within a type-theoretic version of these logics.
.

3.1.
The proof systems
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}

The proof systems considered in this paper are formulated in the sequent calculus style presentation, with
formulas
from second-order multiplicative-exponential linear logic with units (
\mathsf{MELL}_{2}
).
These are generated by a countable set of propositional variables
\mathcal{A}=\{X,Y,\ldots\}
using the following grammar:

A\Coloneqq X\mid{X}^{\bot}\mid A\mathop{\otimes}A\mid A\invamp A\mid\oc A\mid\wn A\mid\mathsf{1}\mid\bot\mid\forall X.A\mid\exists X.A

A
\oc
-formula
(resp.
\wn
-formula
) is a formula of the form
\oc A
(resp.
\wn A
). An
exponential formula
is either a
\oc
-formula or a
\wn
-formula. We denote by
\mathsf{FV}(A)
the set of propositional variables occurring free in
A
, and by
A[B/X]
the standard meta-level capture-avoiding substitution of
B
for the free occurrences of the propositional variable
X
in
A
.

Linear negation

{(\cdot)}^{\bot}
is defined by De Morgan’s laws

{({{A}^{\bot}})}^{\bot}=A
,

{(A\mathop{\otimes}B)}^{\bot}={A}^{\bot}\invamp{B}^{\bot}
,

{(\oc A)}^{\bot}=\wn{A}^{\bot}
,

{(\mathsf{1})}^{\bot}=\bot
, and
{(\forall X.A)}^{\bot}=\exists X.{A}^{\bot}
,
while
linear implication
is
A\multimap B\coloneqq{A}^{\bot}\invamp B
.

)-free

Definition 3.1

(Sequent calculus rules)
.

We consider the sequent calculus rules

axiom
(
\mathsf{ax}
),

cut
(
\mathsf{cut}
),

tensor
(
\mathop{\otimes}
),

par
(
\invamp
),

one
(
\mathsf{1}
),

bottom
(
\bot
),

weakening
(
\wn\mathsf{w}
),

absorption
(
\wn\mathsf{b}
),

functorial promotion
(
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
),

conditional promotion
(
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
),

non-uniform functorial promotion
(
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
),

co-weakening
(
\oc\mathsf{w}
),

co-absorption
(
\oc\mathsf{b}
)

universal quantifier
(
\forall
),

existential quantifier
(
\exists
) from

Figures

2
and
3
.
Rules

\mathop{\otimes}
,
\invamp
,
\mathsf{1}
and
\bot

are
multiplicative
, rules
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
,
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
,
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
,
\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
are
exponential
, rules
\forall
and
\exists
are
second-order
.
The formulas
A
and
{A}^{\bot}
in the rule
\mathsf{cut}
in
Figure

2
are the
cut-formulas
of the rule.
A
\mathsf{cut}
rule is:

•

of shape
\mathsf{r}_{1}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{r}_{2}
if its premises are conclusions of rules
\mathsf{r}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
;

•

multiplicative
if it is of shape
\mathsf{r}_{1}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{r}_{2}
where either at least one among
\mathsf{r}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
is
\mathsf{ax}
, or
\mathsf{r}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
are multiplicative and introduce the cut-formulas;

•

exponential
(resp.
second-order
) if it is of shape
\mathsf{r}_{1}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{r}_{2}
where
\mathsf{r}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
are
exponential
(resp.
second-order
) and introduce the cut-formulas.

Every proof system we will consider is a subset of the rules in
Figures

2
and
3
.

Definition 3.2

(Proof systems
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
)
.

Second-order parsimonious linear logic
, noted
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
, is defined by the set of rules

\{\mathsf{ax},\mathsf{cut},\mathop{\otimes},\invamp,\mathsf{1},\bot,\wn\mathsf{b},\wn\mathsf{w},\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}},\forall,\exists\}
.
The set of derivations over the rules in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
is also denoted by
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
.
The
propositional
fragment of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(both the set of rules and the set of its derivations) is denoted by
\mathsf{PLL}
.

Second-order non-uniform parsimonious linear logic
, noted
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
, is defined by the set of rules
\{\mathsf{ax},\mathsf{cut},\mathop{\otimes},\invamp,\mathsf{1},\bot,\wn\mathsf{b},\wn\mathsf{w},\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p},\forall,\exists\}
, i.e., by replacing
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
with
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
.
The set of derivations over the rules in
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
is also noted
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
.
6
6

6

This requires a slight change in
Definition

2.1
: the tree labelled by a derivation in
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
must be over
\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
instead of
\{1,2\}^{*}
, to deal with infinitely branching derivations.

Note that
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
subsumes
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
. Indeed, rule
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
is simulated by rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
when the derivations
\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots
in its premises are the same.

Remark 3.3
.

The proof systems
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
are inspired by Mazza’s (type) systems
non-uniform parsimonious logic

\mathsf{nuPL}_{\forall\ell}
and
parsimonious logic

\mathsf{PL}_{\forall\ell}
.
The main difference lies in the fact that our proof systems are free of the
co-absorption rule
(
\oc\mathsf{b}
) and the
co-weakening rule
(
\oc\mathsf{w}
) in
Figure

3
, which are not admissible in linear logic.
Because of the absence of these rules,
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
can be properly seen as subsystems of linear logic.

.

Examples of derivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(

Example 3.4
.

Figure

4
gives some examples of derivation in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
.
The (distinct) derivations
\underline{\mathbf{0}}
and
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
prove the same formula
\mathbf{B}=\forall X.\allowbreak({{X}^{\bot}}\invamp{{X}^{\bot}})\invamp({X}\otimes X)
, where
we distinguish the occurrences of the variable
X
by writing them as
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
,
X_{3}
, and
X_{4}
to highlight the differences between the two derivations.

Derivations
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{abs}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{der}}

respectively prove the

absorption law

\oc A\multimap A\mathop{\otimes}\oc A

and

the
dereliction law

\oc A\multimap A
.

Definition 3.5

(Cut elimination steps in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
)
.

The
cut elimination
relation
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(resp.,
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
)
is the union of
multiplicative, second-order and exponential cut elimination steps
in
Figures

5
and
6
(resp.,
Figures

5
and
8
). The non-commutative steps are called
principal
.
The reflexive-transitive closure of
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
is noted
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}
.

Termination of cut elimination in
\mathsf{PLL}
has been proved in
[
ACG24
]
, relying on strong normalization of (second-order) linear logic
[
PTdF10
]
. Hence, the same argument extends straightforwardly to
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
.

Theorem 3.6
.

For every
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
,
there is a cut-free
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
such that
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
).

A byproduct of our grand tour diagram in
Figure

1
is that
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
represents exactly the class of functions in
\mathbf{FP}
. To see this, we introduce a rather permissive notion of representability for
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
, along the lines of
[
GRDR09
,
DLB06
]
.
This notion smoothly adapts to other proof systems we shall study in this paper.

Definition 3.7

(Representability)
.

A set
T
is
represented in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
by a formula
\mathbf{T}
if
there is an injection
(\underline{\,\cdot\,})
from
T
to the set of
\mathsf{cut}
-free derivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
with conclusion
\mathbf{T}
.

A derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}

represents
a (total) function
f\colon T_{1}\times\ldots\times T_{n}\to T
if it proves
\mathbf{T_{1}}\multimap\ldots\multimap\mathbf{T_{n}}\multimap\mathbf{T}
where
\mathbf{T_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{T_{n}},\mathbf{T}
represent
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n},T
respectively, and for all
x_{1}\in T_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in T_{n}
, the reduction in
Figure

7
holds.
A (total) function
f\colon T_{1}\times\ldots\times T_{n}\to T
is
representable in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
if there is a derivation in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
representing
f
.
We denote by
\underline{f}
a derivation representing
f
.

Figure 7
.
Representability of a function
f:T_{1}\times\ldots\times T_{n}\to T
.

Example 3.8
.

The set of Booleans
\mathbb{B}=\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
is represented in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
by the formula
\mathbf{B}
in
Example

3.4
thanks to the derivations
\underline{\mathbf{0}}
and
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
in
Figure

4
.
The set
\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
of Boolean strings is represented by the formula
\mathbf{S}\coloneqq\forall X.\oc(\mathbf{B}\multimap X\multimap X)\multimap X\multimap X
.
We will actually mainly work with a parametric version of
\mathbf{S}
, i.e., the instantiation
\mathbf{S}[A]\coloneqq\oc(\mathbf{B}\multimap A\multimap A)\multimap A\multimap A
for any formula
A
.
We write
\mathbf{S}[]
to denote
\mathbf{S}[A]
for some
A
. Each string
b_{1}\cdots b_{n}\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
with
n\geq 0
is then encoded in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
by the derivation
\underline{b_{1}\cdots b_{n}}
of
\mathbf{S}[A]
shown in
Equation

1
below:

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\underline{b_{1}}}

\mathbf{B}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\underline{b_{n}}}

\mathbf{B}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\otimes\;

{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot},{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\otimes\;

({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),{A}^{\bot},A

\vdots

({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),\overset{n-1}{\ldots},({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\otimes\;

({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),\overset{n-1}{\ldots},({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot},{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\mathop{\otimes}\;

({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),\overset{n}{\ldots},({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\wn\mathsf{w}\;

({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),\overset{n}{\ldots},({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),\wn({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle n\times\wn\mathsf{b}\;

\wn({\mathbf{B}}\otimes{A}\otimes{A}^{\bot}),{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle 2\times\invamp\;

S[A]

(1)

4.
Non-wellfounded Second-Order Parsimonious Linear Logic

4.1.
The non-wellfounded proof system
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}

In
[
ACG24
]
we introduced
\mathsf{PLL}^{\infty}
, a non-wellfounded (finitely branching) version of propositional parsimonious linear logic
\mathsf{PLL}
, by exploiting the notion of coderivation, as opposed to derivation (see
Definition

2.1
).
We now introduce
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, a second-order extension of
\mathsf{PLL}^{\infty}
.

Definition 4.1

(The proof system
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
)
.

Non-wellfounded second-order parsimonious linear logic
, noted
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, is defined as the set of
coderivations
over the set of rules
\{\mathsf{ax},\mathop{\otimes},\invamp,\allowbreak\mathsf{1},\bot,\allowbreak\mathsf{cut},\wn\mathsf{b},\wn\mathsf{w},\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}},\forall,\exists\}
, i.e., obtained from
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(resp.,
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
) by replacing
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
(resp.,
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
) with the
conditional promotion
rule
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
(see
Figure

3
).

Non-wellfounded second-order parsimonious linear logic
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
subsumes both
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
.
Indeed, both
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
and
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
can be simulated in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
by an infinite coderivation called
non-wellfounded box
(see
Figure

9
and the definition below) obtained by iterating
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
(to simulate
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
, the coderivations
\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots
in
Figure

9
have to be the same).

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathcal{D}_{\bot}&\mathcal{D}_{\wn}&\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}\\

.
Examples of coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(
\mathcal{D}_{\bot},\mathcal{D}_{\wn}
) and the non-wellfounded box
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Definition 4.2
.

A
non-wellfounded box

(
nwb
for short)
is a coderivation of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
as in
Figure

9
, for any formula
A
, sequent
\Gamma
and coderivations
\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. Its
principal formula
is the formula
\oc A
; its
main branch
is the infinite branch
\{\epsilon,2,22,\dots\}
, and its
i
-th
call
is the coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{i}
.

Non-wellfounded boxes will range over
\mathfrak{S}
, where
\mathfrak{S}(i)
denotes its
i
-th call, and
\mathsf{Calls}(\mathfrak{S})=\{\mathfrak{S}(i)\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
denotes the set of its calls.

We say that
\mathfrak{S}
has
finite support
(resp. is
periodic
with
period

k
)
if
\mathsf{Calls}(\mathfrak{S})
is finite (resp. if there is a minimal
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathfrak{S}(i)=\mathfrak{S}(k+i)
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
).
A coderivation
\mathcal{D}

has
finite support
(resp. is
periodic
)
if any
nwb
in
\mathcal{D}
has finite support (resp. is periodic).

Example 4.3
.

Streams of booleans can be encoded in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
by
nwb
s

\mathfrak{S}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
as in
Figure

9
with
A\coloneqq\mathbf{B}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}\in\{\underline{\mathbf{0}},\underline{\mathbf{1}}\}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
\mathfrak{S}
has finite support, as its only calls can be
\underline{\mathbf{0}}
or
\underline{\mathbf{1}}
, and it is periodic if and only if so is the stream
(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)\in\{\underline{\mathbf{0}},\underline{\mathbf{1}}\}^{\omega}
.

Definition 4.4

(Cut elimination and representability in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
)
.

The
cut elimination
relation
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is the union of multiplicative, second-order, commutative and exponential cut elimination steps in
Figures

5
and
10
.
The reflexive-transitive closure of
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
is noted
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}
.
The notion of
representability
for
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
can be obtained by adapting
Definition

3.7
to coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
in the obvious way.
Example

4.3
shows that streams of booleans are representable in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Similarly to
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
, the non-wellfounded proof-system
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
admits a computational interpretation based on streams. A stream of data
\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots
of type
A
can be encoded by a
nwb
of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
as in
Figure

9
(see, e.g.,
Example

4.3
).
The cut elimination step
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
then pops the head of the stream, the step
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
erases a stream and, finally,
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
allows us to stepwise “zip” two streams, that is, to create a new
nwb
whose
i
-th call is obtained by cutting the
i
-th calls of the two input
nwb
s.

Remark 4.5
.

Unlike the streams encoded by the rule
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
of
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
,
nwb
s can encode streams with infinitely many distinct entries. As an example, the stream whose
i^{\textup{th}}
entry is the boolean stream
(\underline{\mathbf{1}},\overset{i}{\ldots},\underline{\mathbf{1}},\underline{\mathbf{0}},\dots)
is represented by the
nwb

\mathfrak{S}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\mathcal{D}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n},\ldots)}
as in
Figure

9
with
A\coloneqq\oc\mathbf{B}
and

\mathcal{D}_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{\mathbf{1}},\overset{i}{\ldots},\underline{\mathbf{1}},\underline{\mathbf{0}},\ldots)}
, whose calls are (cut-free and) pairwise distinct.

In
Section

4.4
we will introduce conditions on coderivations of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
enforcing finite support of the stream encoded by a
nwb
by requiring that it has
finitely
many distinct calls.

Figure 10
.
Exponential cut elimination steps in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(with
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
).

4.2.
Totality via the progressivity criterion

The non-wellfounded proof system
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is logically inconsistent, as the coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{\bot}
in
Figure

9
shows that any non-empty sequent is provable in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.
In particular, this coderivation is not
\mathsf{cut}
-free and can only reduce to itself by a cut elimination step, so that cut elimination fails for
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. From a computational viewpoint, this means that
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
can represent non-total functions.

In non-wellfounded proof theory, the typical way to recover logical consistency and (computationally)
totality
of representable functions, is to introduce a global soundness condition on coderivations called
progressing criterion

[
BS11
,
KPP21
,
Das21
,
Das20a
]
.
In
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, this criterion relies on tracking occurrences of
\oc
-formulas in coderivations

[
ACG24
]
.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a coderivation in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.
An occurrence of a formula in a premise of a rule
\mathsf{r}
is the
parent
of an occurrence of a formula in the conclusion if they are connected according to the edges depicted in
Figure

11
.
A
\oc
-thread
in
\mathcal{D}
is a maximal sequence
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\oc
-formulas for some downward-closed
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
A_{i+1}
is the parent of
A_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
A
\oc
-thread
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
is
progressing
if
A_{j}
is in the conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
for infinitely many
j\in I
.

\mathcal{D}
is
progressing
if every infinite branch contains a progressing
\oc
-thread.

Note that every derivation in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is (vacuously) progressing.

Figure 11
.
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
rules: edges connect a formula in the conclusion with its parent(s) in a premise.

Example 4.7
.

The coderivations
\mathcal{D}_{\bot}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\wn}
in
Figure

9
are not progressing:
the rightmost branch of
\mathcal{D}_{\bot}
, i.e., the branch
\{\epsilon,2,22,\ldots\}
,
and the unique branch of
\mathcal{D}_{\wn}

are infinite and contain no
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules.
By contrast, the
nwb

\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{i_{0}},\ldots,\underline{i_{n}},\ldots)}
discussed in
Example

4.3
is progressing since the only infinite branch is its main branch, which contains a
\oc
-thread of formulas
\oc A
, each one principal for a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule. Finally, the regular coderivation below is not progressing:
the branch
\{\epsilon,2,21,212,2121,\dots\}
is infinite but has no progressing
\oc
-thread (where
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
are distinct occurrences of the propositional variable
X
).

Remark 4.8
.

Any infinite branch in a progressing coderivation
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
contains exactly one progressing
\oc
-thread.
This follows from maximality of
\oc
-threads and the fact that conclusions of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules contain at most one
\oc
-formula.
As a consequence, any infinite
\oc
-thread in a branch of
\mathcal{D}
must be progressing.

In
[
ACG24
]
, we proved a cut elimination result for the
propositional
progressing coderivations of
\mathsf{PLL}^{\infty}
(i.e., without second-order), called
continuous cut elimination theorem
. Its proof relies on defining particular infinitary rewriting strategies, showing that the infinite branches of the limit cut-free coderivation constructed are well-defined and contain progressing
\oc
-threads. The proof smoothly extends to the whole
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
thanks to (
\oc,\wn
)-freeness of the formulas instantiated by the rule
\exists
. Indeed, by virtue of that condition, a
\oc
-thread of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
never starts at the active formula of
\exists
: as in the propositional case, it can only start at a formula in the conclusion of the coderivation or at a cut-formula. Therefore, our restricted second-order quantifiers, and the corresponding cut elimination step
\exists\mbox{-vs-}\forall
, do not change the
geometry
of coderivations. As a consequence, the
\mathsf{cut}
admissibility result below holds.

Theorem 4.9

(Cut elimination for progressing
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
)
.

For every progressing coderivation of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
there is a cut-free progressing coderivation with the same conclusion.

4.3.
Approximating coderivations

Rewriting a coderivation to a cut-free one may require infinitely many steps of cut elimination. In this subsection we introduce a notion of approximation for coderivations and show that for
finite
approximations there is a bound on the number of cut elimination steps.

Definition 4.10
.

We define the set of rules
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}\coloneqq\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}\cup\{\mathsf{hyp}\}
, where
\mathsf{hyp}\coloneqq{}\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 13.23883pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 3.125pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 3.125pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to6.25002pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.875pt\hbox{$\Gamma$}\kern 1.875pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 13.23883pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 3.125pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 3.125pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to6.25002pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.875pt\hbox{$\Gamma$}\kern 1.875pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.2672pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.47223pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 2.47223pt}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to4.94446pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.625pt\hbox{$\Gamma$}\kern 1.625pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.61942pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.07642pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 2.07642pt}\kern 0.71759pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8403pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to4.15283pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8403pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 0.71759pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.60419pt\hbox{$\Gamma$}\kern 1.60419pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}$}}
for any sequent
\Gamma
.
We will also refer to
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
as the set of coderivations over
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, which we call
open coderivations
.
An
open derivation
is a derivation in
\mathsf{o}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. Previously introduced notions and definitions on coderivations extend to open coderivations in the obvious way, e.g., the global condition in
Definition

4.6
as well as cut elimination
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
.

Note that there are open coderivations containing
\mathsf{cut}
rules that cannot be further reduced by the cut elimination steps in
Figure

5
and
Figure

10
, since no cut elimination step is defined for
\mathsf{hyp}
. Henceforth, we will call such open coderivations
normal
.

Definition 4.11
.

Let

\mathcal{D}
be an open coderivation and

\mathcal{V}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}\allowbreak\subseteq\{1,2\}^{*}
be a finite set of mutually incomparable nodes of
\mathcal{D}
(w.r.t. the prefix order). If
\{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a set of open coderivations such that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}
has the same conclusion as the sub-coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{v_{i}}
of
\mathcal{D}
, denote by

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}/v_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{n}/v_{n})
,
the open coderivation obtained by replacing each
\mathcal{D}_{v_{i}}
with

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}
in
\mathcal{D}
. The
pruning
of
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{V}

is the open coderivation
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{hyp}/v_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{hyp}/v_{n})
.

If
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are open coderivations,

\mathcal{D}
is an
approximation
of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

(noted
\mathcal{D}\preceq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
)
iff
\mathcal{D}=\llfloor{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\rrfloor_{\mathcal{V}}
for some
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,2\}^{*}
.
An approximation is
finite
if it is an open derivation.

Cut elimination steps do not increase the size of open derivations:

Proposition 4.12

(Cubic bound)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an open derivation and let
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D})
be the maximum number of
\wn
-formulas in the conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule of
\mathcal{D}
.
If

\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\cdots\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{n}

then:

(1)

n\in\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D})^{3}\cdot|\mathcal{D}|^{3})

(2)

|\mathcal{D}_{i}|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D})\cdot|\mathcal{D}|)
for any
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
.

Proof 4.13
.

For
\mathcal{D}
an open derivation, let
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})
be the number of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D})
be the sum of the sizes of all subderivations
of
\mathcal{D}
whose root is the conclusion of a
\mathsf{cut}
rule.
If
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
via:

•

a commutative cut elimination step (
Figure

5
), then
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})=\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
,
|\mathcal{D}|=|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|
and
\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D})>\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
;

•

a multiplicative or second-order cut elimination step (
Figure

5
), then
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})=\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
and
|\mathcal{D}|>|\mathcal{D}^{\prime}|
;

•

an exponential cut elimination step (
Figure

10
), then
and
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})>\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.

Since the lexicographic order over the tuple

(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}),|\mathcal{D}|,\mathsf{H}(\mathcal{D}))

is wellfounded, we conclude that there is no infinite sequence
(\mathcal{D}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
.

Now, let
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\cdots\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{n}
.
First, we show that the number
n_{p}
of its principal cut elimination steps is bounded by
\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D})\cdot\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})+\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D})
,
where
\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D})
is the number of inference rules different from
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathcal{D}
.
This boils down to showing that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
implies
\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})<\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
. Indeed:

•

every cut elimination step cannot increase
\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D})

•

every multiplicative cut elimination step decreases
\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D})
and cannot increase
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})

•

the exponential steps
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
decrease
\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D})
and cannot increase
\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D})

•

if
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}
is obtained by applying a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
step then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})&\coloneqq&\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})\cdot\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})+\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})\\
&\leq&\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\cdot\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime})+(\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})-1+\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}))\\
&=&\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\cdot(\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})-1)+\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})-1+\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\\
&=&\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\cdot\mathsf{C}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})+\mathsf{M}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})-1<\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\\
\end{array}

At the same time,
the number

n^{i}_{c}
of commutative steps performed after the
i
-th principal
is bounded by the square of the maximum size of the proof during rewriting, which can be bounded by
\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})
.
Hence, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n&=&n_{p}+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p}}n^{i}_{c}\leq n_{p}+n_{p}\max_{i}\{n^{i}_{c}\}\\
&\leq&n_{p}\left(\max_{i}\{n^{i}_{c}\}+1\right)\leq\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})\cdot(\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})^{2}+1)\\
&\leq&2\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})^{3}\end{array}

We conclude as
\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{S}(\mathcal{D})\cdot|\mathcal{D}|)
and
|\mathcal{D}_{i}|\leq\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D}_{i})\leq\mathsf{W}(\mathcal{D})
.

Corollary 4.14
.

\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
over open derivations is strongly normalizing and confluent.

Proof 4.15
.

Strong normalisation is a consequence of
Proposition

4.12
. Moreover, since cut elimination
\to_{\mathsf{cut}}
is strongly normalizing over open derivations
and it is locally confluent by inspection of critical pairs, by Newman’s lemma it is also confluent.

4.4.
The proof systems
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}

Starting from
Remark

4.5
, in
[
ACG23
,
ACG24
]
we introduced two proof systems,
\mathsf{wrPLL}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{rPLL}^{\infty}
representing the non-wellfounded proof-theoretic counterparts of
\mathsf{PLL}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}
respectively. Specifically,
\mathsf{wrPLL}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{rPLL}^{\infty}
are obtained from
\mathsf{PLL}^{\infty}
by identifying additional global conditions called
weak regularity
and
finite expandability
. Roughly, weak regularity corresponds to a relaxation of the regularity property (see
Section

2.1
) that allows us to discard those
nwb
s with infinitely many distinct calls, so that only streams with finite support can be encoded. On the other hand, finite expandability discards those infinite branches whose sequents have an unbounded number of
\oc
- and
\wn
-formulas. Indeed,
\mathsf{cut}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
are the only rules that can increase that number (recall that
\exists
can only instantiate
(\wn,\oc)
-free formulas).

In this subsection, we define the second-order versions of
\mathsf{wrPLL}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{rPLL}^{\infty}
.

Definition 4.16
.

[Proof systems
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
]
A coderivation is

weakly regular
if it has only finitely many distinct sub-coderivations
whose conclusions are left premises of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules;
it is

finitely expandable
if any branch contains finitely many
\mathsf{cut}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
rules.

Weakly regular second-order parsimonious logic
, noted
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, is the set of progressing, finitely expandable, and weakly regular coderivations of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Regular second-order parsimonious logic
, noted
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, is the set of progressing, finitely expandable, and regular coderivations of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Remark 4.17
.

Regularity implies weak regularity and the converse fails (see

Example

4.18
below), so
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}\subsetneq\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. A progressing and finitely expandable
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is regular (resp. weakly regular)
if and only if any
nwb
in
\mathcal{D}
is periodic (resp. has finite support).

Example 4.18
.

\mathcal{D}_{\bot}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\wn}
in
Figure

9
are weakly regular (they have no
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rules) but not finitely expandable (their only infinite branch has infinitely many
\mathsf{cut}
or
\wn\mathsf{b}
).
The coderivation in
Remark

4.5
is not weakly regular (it has infinitely many distinct calls).

An example of a weakly regular but not regular coderivation is the
nwb

\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{(\underline{i_{0}},\ldots,\underline{i_{n}},\ldots)}
in
Example

4.3
when the infinite sequence

(i_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{\omega}
is not periodic (see
Remark

4.17
).

By inspecting
Figures

5
and
10
for
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, we prove the following.

Proposition 4.19
.

Cut elimination preserves progressivity, weak-regularity, regularity and finite expandability.
Therefore, if
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{X}
with
\mathsf{X}\in\{\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty},\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}\}
and

\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
, then also
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{X}
.

Akin to linear logic, we can recover a notion of
depth
for coderivations, defined as the maximal number of “nested”
nwb
s.

Definition 4.20

(Nesting and depth)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.
The
nesting level of a rule

\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{D}
is the number of nodes below it that are roots of a call of a
nwb
. The
nesting level of a
nwb
is the nesting level of its bottommost rule. Finally, the
nesting level of a formula occurrence
in
\mathcal{D}
is the nesting level of the rule whose conclusion contains it.

We say that a rule (resp., formula occurrence,
nwb
) is
shallow
if it has nesting level
0
.
The
depth of
\mathcal{D}
, written
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})
, is the supremum of the nesting level of its rules.

Notice that, if
\mathfrak{S}
is a
nwb
with nesting level
n
, then the nesting level of its calls are
n+1
, and the nesting level of the formula occurrences in the main branch of
\mathfrak{S}
is
n
.
Note also that, although the depth of a coderivation can be infinite in general, weakly regular coderivations always have finite depth.

Proposition 4.21

(
[
ACG24
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{D}
is weakly regular then
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
implies
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})\geq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.

4.5.
NL-decidability of
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}

We call a coderivation
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}

weakly progressing
if every infinite branch contains infinitely many right premises of
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rules. As already shown in
[
ACG24
]
for the propositional setting, progressing and weak progressing coincide for finitely expandable coderivations.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be finitely expandable. If
\mathcal{D}
is weakly progressing then any infinite branch contains the main branch of a
nwb
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is progressing if and only if it is weakly progressing.

Proof 4.23
.

Clearly, a progressing coderivation is also weakly progressing. Now,
let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be finitely expandable and weakly progressing, and let
\mathcal{B}
be an infinite branch in
\mathcal{D}
. By finite expandability there is
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathcal{B}
contains no conclusion of a
\mathsf{cut}
or
\wn\mathsf{b}
with height greater than
h
.
Moreover, by weak progressing condition
there is an infinite sequence
h\leq h_{0}<h_{1}<\ldots<h_{n}<\ldots
such that the sequent of
\mathcal{B}
at height
h_{i}
has shape
\wn\Gamma_{i},\oc A_{i}
. By inspecting the rules in
Figure

2
, each such
\wn\Gamma_{i},\oc A_{i}
can be the conclusion of either a
\wn\mathsf{w}
or a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
(with right premise
\wn\Gamma_{i},\oc A_{i}
). So, there is a
k
large enough such that, for any
i\geq k
, only the latter case applies (and, in particular,
\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma
and
A_{i}=A
for some
\Gamma,A
). Therefore,
h_{k}
is the root of a
nwb
. This also shows that
\mathcal{D}
is progressing.

Moreover, by an argument similar to
[
CD22
, Corollary 32]
we have

Proposition 4.24
.

It is
\mathbf{NL}
-decidable if a regular coderivation is in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Proof 4.25
.

A regular coderivation is represented by a finite cyclic graph. By
Lemma

4.22
checking progressivity comes down to checking that no branch has infinitely many occurrences of a particular rule, which in turn reduces to checking acyclicity for this graph (see
[
CD22
]
).
We conclude since checking acyclicity is a well-known
\mathbf{coNL}
problem, and
\mathbf{coNL}=\mathbf{NL}
[
AB09
]
.

Of course a similar decidability result cannot hold for
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, this proof system containing continuously many coderivations, as hinted by the
nwb
depicted in
Example

4.3
.

Figure 12

1.8403pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 0.71759pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn{A}^{\bot},\wn\Gamma,\oc B$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\kern 0.71759pt\hbox{\kern 20.22975pt\hbox to120.6903pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.8403pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 24.41916pt}\kern 0.71759pt\hbox{\kern
