Figure 13
.
Simulation of
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
via
{(\_)}^{\circ}
.

4.6.
Simulation results

We conclude this section by showing that all functions representable in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
are also representable in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
respectively (
Theorem

4.39
). To this end, we first prove that a cut elimination sequence in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
or
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
can be simulated by a
\omega
-long cut elimination sequence in their non-wellfounded counterparts (
Lemma

4.35
).
Theorem

4.39
is then proved by observing that, when derivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
have
\oc
-free conclusion, cut elimination sequences that fully eliminate the cut rule can be, in fact, simulated by
finite
cut elimination sequences on coderivations (
Lemma

4.37
).

We begin with some useful structural properties.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
\mathsf{r}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}\cup\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}\cup\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be a rule such that either
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{cut}
or
\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}},\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p},\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\}
.
If a
\oc
occurs in
\mathsf{r}
, then a
\oc
occurs in its conclusion.

Proof 4.27
.

If
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{cut}
then it is in
\{\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}},\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p},\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\}
by hypothesis, and in all such cases
\mathsf{r}
contains a
\oc
-formula in the conclusion. Otherwise,
\mathsf{r}
is not a
\mathsf{cut}
, and the property follows by inspecting the other rules of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}\cup\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}\cup\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, recalling that instantiation in the
\exists
rule requires
\oc
-freeness.

Lemma 4.28
.

Any cut-free progressing
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
with a
\oc
-free conclusion is a derivation.

Proof 4.29
.

By progressivity, every infinite branch of
\mathcal{D}
would contain a sequent with an occurrence of
\oc
. Since
\mathcal{D}
is cut-free, by repeatedly applying
Lemma

4.26
we have that the conclusion of
\mathcal{D}
must contain an occurrence of
\oc
, contradicting the hypothesis.

Simulation of cut elimination relies on two translations for
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
into their non-wellfounded formulations
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, respectively.

Definition 4.30

(Translation)
.

We define two (conclusion-preserving) translations

(\cdot)^{\circ}\colon\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}\to\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}

and

(\cdot)^{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}\to\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
,
which expand bottom-up the promotion rules
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
and
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}

into
nwb
s as in
Figure

12
, leaving the other rules unchanged.

Note that the images of the translations
(\cdot)^{\circ}
and
(\cdot)^{\bullet}
are in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, respectively, by
Remark

4.17
.

Observe that if
\mathcal{D}_{1}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a cut elimination step of the form
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
then
\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\circ}
can only be obtained from

\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\circ}
by applying infinitely many cut elimination steps in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, as shown in
Figure

13
, and similarly for the cut elimination step
\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{ib}\mathsf{\oc p}
in
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
. Nonetheless, we can show that coderivations of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
with
\oc
-free conclusion can be turned into cut-free coderivations using only
finitely many
cut elimination steps. As a straightforward consequence, we can infer that any function over binary strings representable in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(resp.
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
) is also representable in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(resp.
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
).

To this end, we introduce some definitions inspired by
[
Sau23
]
. In what follows, coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
will be equipped with a
distance

\delta\colon\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}\times\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}\to\mathbb{N}
given by

\delta(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}$;}\\
\min\{2^{-h}\mid\mathcal{D}\text{ and }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\text{ coincide in all nodes up to height }h\}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that this is well defined even when
\mathcal{D}
or
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a derivation.
As well known, this distance forms a complete (ultra)metric space over any set of (binary, possibly infinite) labeled trees, inducing the so-called
tree topology
.
So, sequences of coderivations in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
may have
limits
.

In the next definition, we identify
\mathbb{N}
with the least limit ordinal
\omega
.

Definition 4.31

(
\lambda
-reduction sequence)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
. A
\lambda
-reduction sequence
is a
\lambda
-indexed sequence
\sigma\coloneqq(\mathcal{D}_{i})_{i\in\lambda}
for any
i
such that
i+1\in\lambda
.
The height of the
\mathsf{cut}
rule reduced at the cut elimination step
\mathcal{D}_{i}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
in
\sigma
is denoted by
h_{\sigma}(i)
.
We say that
\sigma
is
height-increasing
if
\lim_{i\in\lambda}h_{\sigma}(i)=\infty
.
It is
weakly converging
if
\lim_{i\in\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{i}
exists; we then write
\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}
to mean that
\lim_{i\in\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}
.
Finally,
\sigma
is
strongly converging
if it is weakly converging and height-increasing.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, any
\lambda
-reduction sequence is weakly converging and not height-increasing.

Definition 4.32

(Splitting function)
.

Given a height-increasing
\omega
-reduction sequence
\sigma
, a strictly monotone function

\ell\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
is a
splitting function (for
\sigma
)
if, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

h_{\sigma}(i)\leq j
for all
i\leq\ell(j)
;

•

h_{\sigma}(i)>j
for all
i>\ell(j)
.

Note that every height-increasing
\omega
-reduction sequence
\sigma
has a splitting function.
Indeed, since
\lim_{i\in\omega}h_{\sigma}(i)=\infty
, for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
there is
n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
i\in\omega
, if
i>n_{j}
then
h_{\sigma}(i)>j
, and
h_{\sigma}(i)\leq j
otherwise;
we can shift the
n_{j}
’s so that the sequence
(n_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly increasing;
a splitting function for
\sigma
is then
\ell\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
defined by
\ell(j)=n_{j}
.

Lemma 4.33

(
\omega
-compression)
.

Let
\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\omega}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
be a strongly converging
\omega
-reduction sequence in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, and let
\ell
be a splitting function for
\sigma
. Then:

(1)

If
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+1}
reduces a cut with height
k
there is a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+1}
such that, if
\lambda=\omega
:

•

\sigma^{\prime}
is strongly converging

•

there is a splitting function
\ell^{\prime}
for
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\sigma(j)=\sigma^{\prime}(j)
for all
j\leq\ell(k)\leq\ell^{\prime}(k)
.

(2)

If
\tau\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\omega}\mathcal{D}_{\omega\cdot 2}
is a strongly converging
\omega
-reduction sequence such that
(h_{\tau}(\omega+i))_{i\in\omega}
is strictly increasing, then there is a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{*}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\omega\cdot 2}
such that, if
\lambda=\omega
then
\sigma^{*}
is strongly converging.

Proof 4.34
.

(1)

Let
\ell
be a splitting function for
\sigma
and let
k
be the height of the cut reduced by the cut elimination step
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+1}
. We have two cases. If the cut elimination step is not of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
then it commutes with any step
\mathcal{D}_{i}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
with
i>\ell(k)
, and so we can construct a strongly converging
\omega
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\omega}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+1}
. Notice that the function
\ell^{\prime}
defined by
\ell^{\prime}(j)\coloneqq\ell(j)+1
for all
j\geq k
and
\ell^{\prime}(j)\coloneqq\ell(j)
otherwise is splitting for
\sigma^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that
\sigma(j)=\sigma^{\prime}(j)
for all
j\leq\ell(k)\leq\ell^{\prime}(k)
. Otherwise, the cut elimination step
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+1}
is of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
. In this case it only
weakly
commutes with any step
\mathcal{D}_{i}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{i+1}
with
i>\ell(k)
. Indeed, a cut elimination step
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
erases possibly infinite inference rules, and so anticipating it in the sequence
\sigma
might prevent infinitely many reduction steps being applied. Therefore, we can construct a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+1}
satisfying the required conditions.

(2)

We have two cases. If by repeatedly applying
Item

1
we obtain a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{\prime}:\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+n}
where
\lambda\in\omega
, then we simply define
\sigma^{*}
by concatenating
\sigma^{\prime}
with
\tau(\omega+n)\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\omega}\mathcal{D}_{\omega\cdot 2}
. Clearly,
\sigma^{*}
is a strongly convergent
\omega
-reduction sequence, and so we are done. Otherwise,

we can construct a family
(\sigma_{n}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\omega}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+n})_{n\in\omega}
of strongly convergent
\omega
-reduction sequences and a family
(\ell_{n})_{n\in\omega}
such that
\ell_{n}
is a splitting function for
\sigma_{n}
. For the base case, we set
\sigma_{0}\coloneqq\sigma
and
\ell_{0}
any splitting function for
\sigma
. Concerning the inductive case,
\sigma_{n+1}
and
\ell_{n+1}
are obtained by applying
Item

1
to
\sigma_{n}
,
\ell_{n}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{\omega+n}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+n+1}
. Note that
\sigma_{n}(j)=\sigma_{n+1}(j)
for all
j\leq\ell_{n}(h_{\tau}(n))\leq\ell_{n+1}(h_{\tau}(n))
by construction of
\sigma_{n+1}
.

We now consider the sequence
\sigma^{*}
of length
\omega
defined as
\sigma^{*}(i)\coloneqq\lim_{n}\sigma_{n}(i)
for all
i\in\omega
. Notice that the limit defining
\sigma^{*}(i)
exists. Indeed, by construction
(\sigma_{n}(i))_{n\in\omega}
is eventually constant.
Indeed, since
(h_{\tau}(n))_{n\in\omega}
is strictly increasing and each
\ell_{n}
is strictly monotone, for every
i\in\omega
there is
n_{0}\in\omega
such that
i\leq\ell_{n_{0}}(h_{\tau}(n_{0}))
. Moreover, by construction
\ell_{n_{0}}(h_{\tau}(n_{0}))\leq\ell_{m}(h_{\tau}(n_{0}))
for every
m\geq n_{0}
and so
\sigma_{m}(i)=\sigma_{n_{0}}(i)
. Hence,
\sigma^{*}(i)=\lim_{n}\sigma_{n}(i)=\sigma_{n_{0}}(i)
. This also shows that
\sigma^{*}
is an
\omega
-reduction sequence.

Moreover, the sequence is weakly converging.
Indeed, we have that
\lim_{i}\sigma^{*}(i)=\lim_{i}\lim_{n}\sigma_{n}(i)=\lim_{n}\lim_{i}\sigma_{n}(i)=\lim_{n}\mathcal{D}_{\omega+n}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega\cdot 2}
. To show that it is also strongly converging we need to prove that it is height-increasing. Notice that by construction, for every
n\in\omega
,
\sigma^{*}
contains a cut elimination step reducing a cut of height
h_{\tau}(n)
. Since by hypothesis
(h_{\tau}(n))_{n\in\omega}
is strictly increasing, it must be that
\lim_{i\in\omega}h_{\sigma^{*}}(i)=\infty
.

Lemma 4.35

(
\omega
-simulation)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a derivation of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(resp.,
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
). If
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
then there is a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\circ}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\circ}
(resp.,
\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\bullet}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\bullet}
) with
\lambda\leq\omega
. Moreover, if
\lambda=\omega
then the sequence is strongly converging.

Proof 4.36
.

We prove the statement by induction on the length of
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. We only consider the case where
\mathcal{D}
is a derivation of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
, as the case for
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
can be treated similarly. If
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
then we are done. Otherwise, we have
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. By induction hypothesis, there is a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\circ}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\circ}
satisfying the conditions. We do case analysis on
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. If the cut elimination step reduces a cut
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
then it is easy to check that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\circ}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\circ}
. So, if
\lambda=\omega
then
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
is strongly converging, and we can apply
Lemma

4.33
.
1
. Otherwise,
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
and we simulate
\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by an
\omega
-reduction sequence
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{\prime\prime\circ}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\omega}\mathcal{D}^{\prime\circ}
as in
Figure

13
. It is easy to see that
\sigma^{\prime}
can be constructed as a strongly converging
\omega
-reduction sequence and, in particular, such that
(h_{\sigma^{\prime}}(i))_{i\in\omega}
is strictly increasing. So, if
\lambda=\omega
we conclude by applying
Lemma

4.33
.
2
.

Lemma 4.37

(Finite simulation)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a derivation of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
(resp.,
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
) with
\oc
-free conclusion. If
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
with
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
cut-free, then
\mathcal{D}^{\circ}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}
(resp.,
\mathcal{D}^{\bullet}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{\bullet}
).

Proof 4.38
.

We only consider the case where
\mathcal{D}
is a derivation of
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
, as the case for
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
can be treated similarly. By
Lemma

4.35
we obtain a
\lambda
-reduction sequence
\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\circ}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{\lambda}\hat{\mathcal{D}}^{\circ}
where
\lambda\leq\omega
such that, if
\lambda=\omega
then
\sigma
is strongly converging. Since
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
is cut-free and has
\oc
-free conclusion, then it is a derivation by
Lemma

4.28
. This implies that
\lambda<\omega
. Indeed, if
\lambda=\omega
then
\sigma
would be strongly converging, and so height-increasing. But then
\lim_{i}h_{\sigma}(i)=\infty
, which contradicts finiteness of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
.

Theorem 4.39

(Simulation)
.

Let
f\colon(\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*})^{n}\to\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
.

(1)

If
f
is representable in
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
,
then so it is in
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

(2)

If
f
is representable in
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
, then so it is in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Proof 4.40
.

We only prove
Item

1
, as
Item

2
is proven similarly. Let
\mathcal{D}:\mathbf{S}[]\multimap\overset{n\geq 0}{\ldots}\multimap\mathbf{S}[]\multimap\mathbf{S}
represent
f\colon(\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*})^{n}\to\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
in
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
and let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
. This means that the reduction in
Figure

7
holds for
\mathbf{T_{1}}=\ldots=\mathbf{T}_{n}=\mathbf{S}[]
and
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{S}
. Let
\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{1}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\ldots\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{n}=\underline{f(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})}
be such a reduction sequence, and notice that
\underline{f(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})}
is cut-free by definition.
We conclude by applying
Lemma

4.37
, observing that
\underline{s}^{\bullet}=\underline{s}
for any binary string
s\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
.

5.
Soundness

In this section we prove the soundness theorem, which states that every function
f
over binary strings representable by coderivations of
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(resp.
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
) is in
\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
(resp.
\mathbf{FP}
). Soundness is a straightforward consequence of a polynomial modulus of continuity result for cut elimination (
Lemma

5.25
), according to which we can extract a family of polynomial size circuits
C_{f}
computing
f
. We conclude by observing that
C_{f}
is, in fact, P-uniform whenever the coderivation representing
f
is regular.

5.1.
Shallow cut elimination strategy

Eliminating cuts in coderivations of
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
typically requires infinitary rewriting (see, e.g.,
[
ACG24
]
). However, if we focus on coderivations with
\oc
-free conclusion we can define a cut elimination strategy, we call it
shallow
, that always halt after a
finite
number of steps. This restricted form of cut elimination is enough for establishing soundness, in that computation over binary strings can be duly simulated by reducing cuts in such coderivations.

The so-called
shallow cut elimination strategy
for
\oc
-free coderivations of
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
is divided into rounds, each one divided into two phases. Phase 1 reduces all shallow cuts (i.e., those cuts with nesting level
0
) that do not involve
\textsf{nwb}s
. The shallow cuts affecting
\textsf{nwb}s
, called
steady cuts
, are dealt with in Phase 2. This phase reduces hereditarily all steady cuts produced by Phase 1 except those of the form
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
(as reducing the latter would “merge two boxes” and can be avoided thanks to
Proposition

5.12
.
2
).

Definition 5.1

(Steady cuts and
\textsf{nwb}s
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. A
steady cut
is a shallow cut with an active formula that is
\oc
-principal for a (shallow)
nwb

\mathfrak{S}
. We call
\mathfrak{S}
a
steady
nwb
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be a coderivation of a
\oc
-free sequent whose shallow cuts are all steady. Then:

(1)

All shallow
\textsf{nwb}s
are all steady.

(2)

There is a cut
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
.

Proof 5.3
.

The proof of
Item

1
is analogous to
Lemma

4.28
. If there is a shallow
nwb

\mathfrak{S}

that is not steady and all shallow cuts are steady then, by
Lemma

4.26
, the conclusion of
\mathcal{D}
contains a
\oc
, which contradicts the hypothesis.

Concerning
Item

2
, by
Item

1
there are exactly
n
steady cuts and
n
steady
\textsf{nwb}s
. By the tree structure of
\mathcal{D}
there must be a shallow cut that with an active formula that is not in the conclusion of a shallow
nwb
.

Definition 5.4

(Shallow rewriting strategy)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be a coderivation of a
\oc
-free sequent. The
shallow cut elimination strategy
iterates
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
times the two phases below
7
7

7

To make the strategy deterministic, we can give priority to the rightmost reducible
\mathsf{cut}
with smallest height. This would ensure that the strategy eventually applies a cut elimination step to every reducible cut.
:

•

Phase 1.
Reduce all shallow cuts that are not steady.

•

Phase 2.
Fully reduce all steady cuts
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
except those that become shallow during this phase.

We call each iteration of the two phases above a
round
. We set
\mathcal{D}^{0}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}
and, for all
1\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
,
\mathcal{D}^{d}
to be the coderivation obtained after the
d
-th round. For all
1\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
we set
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}
as the coderivation obtained from
\mathcal{D}^{d-1}
by applying
Phase 1
. We write

{\mathcal{D}^{d}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{m}}{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d+1}}
to denote that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d+1}
has been obtained in a finite number of steps from

\mathcal{D}^{d}
by applying
Phase 1
;
{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{e}}{\mathcal{D}^{d}}
to denote that

\mathcal{D}^{d}
has been obtained in a finite number of steps from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}

by applying
Phase 2
; finally, we set
{\mathcal{D}^{d}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{r}}{\mathcal{D}^{d+1}}\coloneqq{{\mathcal{D}^{d}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{m}}{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d+1}}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{e}}{\mathcal{D}^{d+1}}
.

In the next subsections we will introduce the technical definitions and results to prove that the shallow cut elimination strategy applied to coderivations with
\oc
-free conclusion always terminates. The idea behind termination is that after each round all steady
\textsf{nwb}s
are eventually erased, so that the depth of the coderivation decreases by 1. This happens because during each round all steady cuts will eventually be reduced to a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
cut.

5.2.
Decomposition prebar and truncations

As already noticed in
[
ACG23
,
ACG24
]
in a propositional setting, thanks to finite expandability and (weak) regularity, coderivations of
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
can be “decomposed” into a finite tree together with a finite number of
nwb
s. Such a decomposition property will allow an inductive description of coderivations for our non-wellfounded proof systems.

Recalling
Definition

2.2
, the following is a straightforward consequence of
Lemma

4.22
:

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. There is a prebar
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,2\}^{*}
of
\mathcal{D}

such that each
v\in\mathcal{V}
is the root of a
nwb
.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.
The
decomposition prebar

of
\mathcal{D}
is the
minimal prebar
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{D}
such that, for all
v\in\mathcal{V}
,

\mathcal{D}_{v}
is a
nwb
. We denote with
\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})
such a prebar and we set

\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})\coloneqq\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})}
.

Remark 5.7
.

If
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
then, by weak König’s lemma,
\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})
is finite and
\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})
is a finite approximation of
\mathcal{D}
.

The
n
-truncation
of a coderivation
\mathcal{D}
is a particular finite approximation of
\mathcal{D}
that only considers the first
n
calls of each
nwb
. Similarly, an
n
-hypertruncation
only considers the first
n
-calls of the shallow
nwb
s of
\mathcal{D}
.

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{0}}

\Gamma,A

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{1}}

\Gamma,A

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}_{n-1}}

\Gamma,A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;

\Gamma,A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{hyp}\;

\wn\Gamma,\oc A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\wn\Gamma,\oc A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\ddots

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\wn\Gamma,\oc A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\wn\Gamma,\oc A

Figure 14
.
A finite non-wellfounded promotion.

Definition 5.8

(Finite
\textsf{nwb}s
and truncations)
.

A
finite non-wellfounded promotion
is defined as a coderivation
\mathfrak{F}={\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{\langle\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{n-1}\rangle}}
in
Figure

14
.
We write
\mathfrak{F}(i)
to denote
\mathcal{D}_{i}
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
with
\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
(so
\mathfrak{S}_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{v_{i}}
is a
nwb
for all
1\leq i\leq k
).
The
n
-truncation

\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}
and the
n
-hypertruncation

\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{n}
of
\mathcal{D}
are the open derivations defined for all
n>0
as follows: if
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})=0
, then
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}=\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{n}\coloneqq\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})=\mathcal{D}
, and if
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})>0
, then

\displaystyle\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})(\vec{\mathfrak{F}_{i}}/\vec{v_{i}})

\displaystyle\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})(\vec{\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}_{i}}/\vec{v_{i}})

where for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
\mathfrak{F}_{i}={\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{\langle\llfloor{\mathfrak{S}_{i}(0)}\rrfloor_{n},\ldots,\llfloor{\mathfrak{S}_{i}(n-1)}\rrfloor_{n}\rangle}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\prime}={\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}_{\langle\mathsf{base}(\mathfrak{S}_{i}(0)),\ldots,\mathsf{base}(\mathfrak{S}_{i}(n-1))\rangle}}
.

Notice that
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}
and
\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{n}
are finite, and
\lfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rfloor_{n}\preceq\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}
.

5.3.
Exponential flows

We now introduce the
exponential graph
of a coderivation
\mathcal{D}
, a directed graph associated to
\mathcal{D}
that allows a static analysis of the cut elimination steps reducing steady cuts. Directed paths of this graph, called
exponential flows
, can be then used to precompute the maximum number of calls of a shallow
nwb
that eventually become shallow as a consequence of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
cut elimination step. This number will be called
rank
of
\mathcal{D}
, and plays a crucial role for establishing a polynomial bound on cut elimination.

Definition 5.9

(Exponential flow)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. The
exponential graph of
\mathcal{D}
, written
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
, is a finite directed forest
8
8

8

A directed forest is a directed acyclic graph whose underlying undirected graph is a forest.
whose nodes are (labelled by) the shallow exponential formulas in
\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})
and whose directed edges connect a node
A
to a node
B
if:

•

A=\wn{C}^{\bot}
and
B=\oc C
are the conclusions of an
\mathsf{ax}
rule;

•

A=\wn{C}^{\bot}
and
B=\oc C
are conclusions of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule;

•

A=\wn C
is principal for a
\wn\mathsf{b}
-rule with active formula
B=\wn C
;

•

A=\oc{C}
and
B=\wn{C}^{\bot}
are the cut-formulas of a
\mathsf{cut}
rule;

•

A=B
, where
A
is a non-principal
\wn
-formula in the conclusion of a rule
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
, and
B
is the corresponding non-active
\wn
-formula in a premise of
\mathsf{r}
.

•

A=B
, where
B
is a non-active
\oc
-formula in a premise of a rule
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
, and
A
is the corresponding non-principal
\oc
-formula in the conclusion of
\mathsf{r}
.

A
\oc
-node
(resp.
\wn
-node
) is a node labelled by a
\oc
-formula (resp.
\wn
-formula).
A
\mathsf{b}
-node
(resp.
\mathsf{w}
-node
) is a node labelled by the principal formula for a
\wn\mathsf{b}
rule (resp. for a
\wn\mathsf{w}
rule).

Directed paths of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
range over
\phi,\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime},\ldots
. Maximal directed graphs of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
will be called
exponential flows
. We say that a directed path
\phi

crosses
an exponential
\mathsf{cut}
rule when it crosses (both of its) active formulas. The
rank of a directed path

\phi
, written
\mathsf{rk}({\phi})
, is the number of
\mathsf{b}
-nodes crossed by
\phi
. Finally, the
rank of
\mathcal{D}
, written
\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}})
, is the number
\mathsf{b}
-nodes of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
.

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

{A}^{\bot},A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

{A}^{\bot},A

\vdots

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\wn{A}^{\bot},\oc A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\wn{A}^{\bot},\oc A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

g

a
l
e
f
h
i

Figure 15
.
A coderivation
\mathcal{D}
and its exponential graph
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
.

Example 5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the coderivation in
Figure

15
. Notice that the sub-coderivation
\mathcal{D}_{1}
(i.e., the one with conclusion the right premise of the
\mathsf{cut}
rule) is a steady
nwb

\mathfrak{S}
whose calls are axioms. There is only one exponential flow in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
, which is
\phi\coloneqq abcdefghil
. We have
\mathsf{rk}({\phi})=\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}})=1
. Finally,
\mathcal{D}
contains only one
\mathsf{cut}
which is shallow and steady.

As we mentioned before, exponential flows can be seen as static representations of cut elimination steps applied to steady cuts. We now study their invariance properties under rewriting. First, we need a notion of residue of cut rules and exponential flows along a cut elimination step.

Definition 5.11

(Residues)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}
be a steady cut. The
residue of
\mathsf{r}
(along
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
)
is the cut rule
\hat{\mathsf{r}}
of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
defined as follows:

•

if
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
does not reduce
\mathsf{r}
then
\hat{\mathsf{r}}\coloneqq\mathsf{r}

•

if
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
reduces
\mathsf{r}
and
\mathsf{r}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
then
\hat{\mathsf{r}}
is the
unique steady
cut that is obtained by reducing
\mathsf{r}
(see
Figure

10
).

•

otherwise,
\hat{\mathsf{r}}
is undefined.

We denote by
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}
an exponential flow that crosses
\mathsf{r}
. We call the
residue of
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}
(along
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
)
the
unique
exponential flow of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
defined by
\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}\coloneqq\phi_{\widehat{\mathsf{r}}}
.

Proposition 5.12

(Invariance)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
with
\oc
-free conclusion where all shallow cuts are steady. Then:

(1)

Every exponential flow
\phi
of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
ends at a
\mathsf{w}
-node.

(2)

If
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
reduces a steady cut
\mathsf{r}^{*}
then, for any steady cut
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathsf{rk}({\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}})\leq\mathsf{rk}({\phi_{\mathsf{r}}})
holds whenever
\mathsf{r}^{*}\neq\mathsf{r}
or
\mathsf{r}^{*}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
.

Proof 5.13
.

Let
\phi
be an exponential flow of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
. We show that:

(1)

every
\wn
-node that has no directed edge to another
\wn
-node is either a
\mathsf{w}
-node at the conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rule.

(2)

every
\oc
-node with no directed edge to another
\oc
-node is the active formula of a
\mathsf{cut}
rule.

The Point (a) follows by inspecting the definition of exponential flow. Concerning Point (b), let
v
be a
\oc
-node
v
with no directed edge to another
\oc
-node. Since all cuts are steady and the conclusion of
\mathcal{D}
is
\oc
-free, by
Lemma

4.26
,
v
must be the
\oc
-active formula of a
\mathsf{cut}
-rule.

We now show that
Item

1
follows from the two points above. Indeed, by maximality of exponential flows, if
\phi
crosses a
\wn
-principal formula in the conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
-rule (resp. the
\wn
-active formula of a
\mathsf{cut}
rule), then it also crosses its
\oc
-principal conclusion (resp. its
\oc
-active formula). This means that, since exponential flows are finite,
\phi
must end at a
\mathsf{w}
-node.

Let us now prove
Item

2
. Let
\mathcal{\phi}
be an exponential flow
\mathcal{\phi}
of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
. If
\mathsf{r}^{*}
is not crossed by
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}
then
\mathsf{rk}({\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}})=\mathsf{rk}({\phi_{\mathsf{r}}})
. Otherwise,
\mathsf{r}^{*}
is crossed by
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}
, and we proceed by case analysis. The cases where
\mathsf{r}
is a commuting cut or a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
cut are straightforward. Suppose now
\mathsf{r}
is a cut
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
. Since by assumption
\mathsf{r}^{*}\neq\mathsf{r}
,
\hat{\mathsf{r}}
is defined, and so is
\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}
. By inspecting
Figure

16
(top) we conclude that
\mathsf{rk}({\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}})\leq\mathsf{rk}({\phi_{\mathsf{r}}})
.
Finally, we consider the case where
\mathsf{r}^{*}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
as in
Figure

16
(bottom). Then,
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}=\phi^{\prime}x_{i}z_{i}ab\phi^{\prime\prime}
for some
i
and for some directed paths
\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}
. We notice that the existence of the directed edge labelled with
c
in the figure is inferred from the fact that
\mathsf{r}^{*}
is steady by hypothesis. This means that
\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}=\phi^{\prime}u_{i}v_{i}cde\phi^{\prime\prime}
. But then
\mathsf{rk}({\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}}}})=\mathsf{rk}({\phi^{\prime}})+\mathsf{rk}({\phi^{\prime\prime}})+1\leq\mathsf{rk}({\phi_{\mathsf{r}}})
.

Figure 16
.
From top, a cut elimination step
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
reducing
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{b}
, and the corresponding exponential graphs (only the relevant nodes and edges are displayed). Double circles (resp., double edges) represent multiple nodes (resp., multiple edges), while squared nodes are nodes shared by
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D})
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
. Edges are labelled by letters, and vectors
\vec{x}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
represent a list of labels, one for each edge. Finally, the dashed edge labelled by
c
exists if
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a
nwb
, i.e., if the cut is steady.

5.4.
Termination theorem

Termination of the shallow cut elimination strategy is an immediate consequence of the Key Lemma (
Lemma

5.16
). To this end, we introduce a partial ordering on steady cuts, denoted
\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}
, which relates two cuts
\mathsf{r}
and
\mathsf{r}
connected by an exponential flow
\phi
in a coderivation
\mathcal{D}
. Intuitively, the
distance
of a cut
\mathsf{r}
to the end node of
\phi
, called
weight of
\mathsf{r}
, is a measure of the number of cut elimination steps required to fully reduce
\mathsf{r}
. Reducing a cut
\mathsf{r}
strictly decreases such a distance for
\mathsf{r}
, while it might increase it for smaller cuts
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}\mathsf{r}
. Termination for shallow cut elimination can be then proven by induction on a lexicographic ordering defined on
\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}
. The Key Lemma will also provide an estimation of the number of calls of a shallow
nwb
that become shallow after each round, which depends on the rank of exponential flows and the fact that the latter cannot increase during cut elimination.

Definition 5.14

(Partial ordering and weight)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. We define a partial ordering on steady cuts
\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}
defined by
\mathsf{r}\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
if only only if there is a directed path from the active formulas of
\mathsf{r}
to those of
\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
. We set
\prec_{\mathcal{D}}
as the strict version of
\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Let
\mathsf{r}
be a steady cut of
\mathcal{D}
. The
weight of
\mathsf{r}
(in
\mathcal{D}
)
, written
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r})
, is the number of nodes in
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}
from the
\wn
-active formula of
\mathsf{r}
to its end node.

The following properties are straightforward from the above definition, the definition of residue, and
Proposition

5.12
.
2
:

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, and let
\mathsf{r},\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
be steady cuts. Then:

(1)

\mathsf{r}\prec_{\mathcal{D}}\mathsf{r}^{\prime}
implies
\phi_{\mathsf{r}}=\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r})>\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{\prime})
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{D}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a cut elimination step reducing a steady cut
\mathsf{r}^{*}\not\in\{\mathsf{r},\mathsf{r}^{\prime}\}
such that
\mathsf{r}^{*}\neq\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
then
\hat{\mathsf{r}}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{\prime}}
exist and
\hat{\mathsf{r}}\preceq_{\mathcal{D}}\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{\prime}}
.

We can now prove the key lemma for the termination of shallow cut elimination strategies.

Lemma 5.16

(Key Lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
with
\oc
-free conclusion. Then, the shallow cut elimination strategy applied to
\mathcal{D}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Phase 1
terminates (i.e.,
{{\mathcal{D}^{d-1}}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{m}}{{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}}
). In particular,
{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})}
for every
1\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
.

(2)

Phase 2
terminates (i.e.,
{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*\mathsf{e}}{\mathcal{D}^{d}}
). In particular,
{\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d})}
for every
1\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
.

(3)

If
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})>0
then

\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d})=\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})-1
.

Proof 5.17
.

Item

1
follows from the fact that shallow cuts that are not steady only affect
\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})
, so that

{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})}
by
Proposition

4.12
.

Let us prove
Item

2
. First, we show that
Phase 2
terminates. Notice that after
Phase 1
all shallow cuts are steady, so
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}
contains only steady cuts. Let
\mathcal{D}_{n}
be the
n
-th step of cut elimination of this phase (so
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}
). We prove the statement by induction on the lexicographical ordering
L(\mathcal{D}_{n})\coloneqq(n^{s},\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{n^{s}}))
where:

•

n^{s}
is the number of steady cuts of
\mathcal{D}_{n}
(except the new ones produced during this phase).

•

\mathsf{r}^{n}_{1}\prec_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}^{l}\ldots\prec^{l}_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathsf{r}^{n}_{n^{s}}
is a (strict) linear ordering that extends the strict ordering
\prec_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}

Now, suppose that
\mathcal{D}_{n}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{n+1}
reduces a steady cut
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
for some
i_{0}
. We have two cases:

•

If
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
then
{(n+1)}^{s}<n^{s}
. This means that
L(\mathcal{D}_{n-1})<L(\mathcal{D}_{n})
.

•

Otherwise, all steady cuts have a steady residue. Since the linear ordering is preserved by
Proposition

5.15
.
2
, we have
\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}}=\mathsf{r}_{i}^{n+1}
, and so
{(n+1)}^{s}=n^{s}
. Moreover, since
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i})>\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i+1})
by
Proposition

5.15
.
1
, we also have
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n+1}_{i})>\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n+1}_{i+1})
for all
i
. By inspecting the commuting and exponential cut elimination rules (see
Figures

5
and
10
), we notice that
\mathcal{D}_{n}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{n+1}
decreases the weight of the residue of
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n+1}_{i_{0}})=\mathsf{wt}(\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}})<\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}})
. Moreover, that reduction step can only increase the weight of the residue of steady cuts
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}\preceq_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
. So, for any
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}
with
i\neq i_{0}
:

–

if
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}
and
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
are incomparable w.r.t.
\preceq_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
, we have

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n+1}_{i})=\mathsf{wt}(\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}})=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i})

–

If
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}\preceq_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}
, we have again

\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n+1}_{i})=\mathsf{wt}(\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}})=\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i})

–

otherwise
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}\preceq_{\mathcal{D}_{n}}\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
, in which case
\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n+1}_{i})=\mathsf{wt}(\widehat{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}})\geq\mathsf{wt}(\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i})
.

This shows that
L(\mathcal{D}_{n-1})<L(\mathcal{D}_{n})
.

Therefore,
Phase 2
terminates. We now show that
{\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d})}
, for every
1\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
. For every
n
, let
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
be the steady cut reduced by
\mathcal{D}_{n}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{n+1}
, and let
\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}}
be the exponential flow of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{D}_{n})
that crosses
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
. If
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}=\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
then
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}
has no residue. Otherwise, by
Proposition

5.12
.
2
we have
\mathsf{rk}({\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}}}})\leq\mathsf{rk}({\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}}})
. Moreover, for every other
\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}
such that
\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i}}\neq\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}}
, we have
\mathsf{rk}({\widehat{\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}}}})=\mathsf{rk}({\phi_{\mathsf{r}^{n}_{i_{0}}}})
. Therefore, only the first
\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})
calls of every shallow
nwb
in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}
can become shallow at the end of the round. Since only shallow rules are affected by
Phase 2
, this implies
{\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d})}
.

Let us finally show
Item

3
. Suppose
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})>0
.
Phase 1
does not affect the depth of
\mathcal{D}^{d-1}
, so
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})=\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})
. Let us consider
Phase 2
. Let
\mathfrak{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{S}_{k}
be the shallow
\textsf{nwb}s
of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}
. Since we assumed
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})=\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})>0
, we have
k>0
. By hypothesis there are only steady cuts, and so all such
\textsf{nwb}s
are steady by
Proposition

5.2
.
1
. Since every steady cut is crossed by an exponential flow, and by
Proposition

5.12
.
1
every exponential flow ends at a
\mathsf{w}
-node, the weight of every steady cut during this phase will eventually decrease to
0
. This means that every steady
nwb
will eventually be erased by reducing a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}\mbox{-vs-}\wn\mathsf{w}
cut. Therefore, after
Phase 2
the depth decreases by
1
, i.e.,
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d})=\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})-1=\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})-1
.

Theorem 5.18

(Termination)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
with
\oc
-free conclusion. The shallow cut elimination strategy applied to
\mathcal{D}
terminates in a
finite
number of steps into a cut-free
derivation
.

Proof 5.19
.

Termination is a consequence of
Lemma

5.16
, as by
Proposition

4.21
the depth of
\mathcal{D}
is finite and reduces at every round. In particular, by
Lemma

5.16
.
3
we have

\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})})=0
and so
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}

is cut-free and
nwb
-free. Hence, by
Remark

5.7
,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}=\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1})
is finite, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}
is a derivation.

5.5.
Polynomial modulus of continuity

In this final subsection, we analyse the complexity of the shallow cut elimination strategy, leveraging the estimations provided by the Key Lemma (
Lemma

5.16
). From the polynomial modulus of continuity result (
Lemma

5.25
), we will infer soundness for our non-wellfounded proof systems (
Theorem

5.27
).

We start with introducing a notion of (finite) size for coderivations in
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, called
cosize
, relying on
Proposition

4.21
.

Definition 5.20

(Cosize)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
, and
\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}
be its decomposition prebar (thus
\mathfrak{S}_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{v_{i}}
is a
nwb
for all
1\leq i\leq k
). We define the
cosize of
\mathcal{D}
, written
||{\mathcal{D}}||
, by induction on
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})
.
If
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})=0
then
\mathcal{D}=\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})
and we set
||{\mathcal{D}}||\coloneqq|\mathcal{D}|
. Otherwise
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})>0
, and

||{\mathcal{D}}||\coloneqq|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Calls}(\mathfrak{S}_{i})}||{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}||
.

The
cosize at depth
d
, written
||{\mathcal{D}}||_{d}
, is defined for all
d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})

as

||{\mathcal{D}}||_{0}=|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})|
,
and
as
||{\mathcal{D}}||_{d+1}=\max\{||{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}||_{d}\mid\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Calls}(\mathfrak{S}_{i})\mbox{ for some }1\leq i\leq k\}
.

Notice that, by
Remark

4.17
,

\mathsf{Calls}(\mathfrak{S}_{i})

is a
finite
set for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, and so
||{\mathcal{D}}||_{d}\leq||{\mathcal{D}}||\in\mathbb{N}
.

The following relation between the size of the
n
-truncation of
\mathcal{D}
, i.e.,
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}
(
Definition

5.8
), and the cosize of
\mathcal{D}
holds.

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.
Then

|\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}|\in\mathcal{O}(n^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1})\qquad.

Proof 5.22
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{v_{i}}
with
v_{i}\in\mathsf{border}(\mathcal{D})
.
If
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})=0
, then
|\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}|=|\mathcal{D}|=||{\mathcal{D}}||
.
If
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})=d+1
, then
|\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}|\coloneqq|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D})|+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{n}|\llfloor{\mathfrak{S}_{i}(j)}\rrfloor_{n}|
by definition.
By the induction hypothesis
|\llfloor{\mathfrak{S}_{i}(j)}\rrfloor_{n}|\in\mathcal{O}(n^{d}\cdot||{\mathfrak{S}_{i}(j)}||^{d})
, hence
|\llfloor{\mathfrak{S}_{i}(j)}\rrfloor_{n}|\in\mathcal{O}(n^{d}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{d})
.
Since
k\leq||{\mathcal{D}}||
, then we have
|\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}|\in\mathcal{O}(||{\mathcal{D}}||+n\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||\cdot n^{d}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{d})
,
and we conclude that

|\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{n}|\in\mathcal{O}(n^{d+1}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{d+1})
.

We now show that shallow cut elimination requires a number of steps that can be polynomially bounded w.r.t. the cosize of the starting coderivation. First, we need a preliminary lemma.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be a coderivation of a
\oc
-free sequent. Then,

||{\mathcal{D}^{d}}||_{0}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d}\left(||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{i}\right)^{2^{d+1-i}}\right)

for all
0\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
.

Proof 5.24
.

First, we notice that, since
\mathcal{D}
is weakly regular and progressing, there is a bound
s^{*}\geq 0
on the maximum number of
\wn
-formulas in the conclusion of a
\mathsf{c\mathsf{\oc p}}
rule of
\mathcal{D}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D})
(see
Proposition

4.12
). Moreover, by
Proposition

4.19
, we can assume that
s^{*}\geq 0
bounds
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D}^{d})
. Hence
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{D}^{d})
will be considered as
constants
throughout this proof.

We prove the statement by induction on
0\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
. The case
d=0
is trivial. If
d>0
then, by
Lemma

5.16
.
2
, we have
{\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d+1})}
. Then by
Proposition

4.12
.
2
:

||{\mathcal{D}^{d}}||_{0}=|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d})|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S}(\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})})\cdot|\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}|)=\mathcal{O}(|\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}|)

(2)

Moreover, if
\mathfrak{S}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are the shallow
nwb
s of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}
, since
n,\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})\leq||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}
and
\mathsf{base}(\mathfrak{S}_{i}(j))\leq||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{1}
, then

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\lfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rfloor_{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})}|&=&|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})|+\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d+1}})}|\mathsf{base}(\mathfrak{S}_{i}(j))|\\
&\in&\mathcal{O}\left(||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}+\left(||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}\right)^{2}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{1}\right)\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(\left(||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}\right)^{2}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{1}\right)\end{array}

(3)

On the other hand:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}&=&|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})|\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})\cdot\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1}))&\mathrm{Prop.}~\ref{thm:cut-elimApp}.\ref{enum:cubic2}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S}(\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d-1})\cdot||{\mathcal{D}^{d-1}}||_{0})\\
&=&\mathcal{O}(||{\mathcal{D}^{d-1}}||_{0})\end{array}

(4)

Finally, we notice that:

||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{1}=||{\mathcal{D}^{d-1}}||_{1}=||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{d}

(5)

as by
Theorem

5.18
.
2
the rules of
\mathcal{D}^{0}
with nesting level
d
are unaffected in the first
d-1
rounds of cut elimination, and by
Theorem

5.18
.
3
each round decreases the depth.
Then, by inductive hypothesis we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}||{\mathcal{D}^{d}}||_{0}&\in&\mathcal{O}\left(\left(||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}\right)^{2}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{1}\right)&\mathrm{Eq.}~\ref{eqn:a}\textrm{-}\ref{eqn:b}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(\left(||{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}||_{0}\right)^{2}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{d}\right)&\mathrm{Eq.}~\ref{eqn:d}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(\left(||{\mathcal{D}^{d-1}}||_{0}\right)^{2}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{d}\right)&\mathrm{Eq.}~\ref{eqn:c}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\left(||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{i}\right)^{2^{d-i}}\right)^{2}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{d}\right)&\textrm{Induction hypothesis}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\left(||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{i}\right)^{2^{d+1-i}}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{d}\right)\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d}\left(||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||_{i}\right)^{2^{d+1-i}}\right)\;.\end{array}

Lemma 5.25

(Polynomial modulus of continuity)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
be a coderivation of a
\oc
-free sequent. Then, for some polynomial
p:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
depending solely on
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})
,
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{p(||{\mathcal{D}}||)}
rewrites by the shallow cut elimination strategy to a cut-free
\mathsf{hyp}
-free derivation.

Proof 5.26
.

By
Lemma

5.23
we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}||{\mathcal{D}^{d}}||_{0}\in&\mathcal{O}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d}||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||^{2^{d+1-i}}_{i}\right)&\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:pre-computing-modulus}}\\
=&\mathcal{O}\left(\prod_{i=0}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})}||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||^{2^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1-i}}_{i}\right)&\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem:depth}}\\
=&\mathcal{O}\left(||{\mathcal{D}^{0}}||^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})\cdot 2^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}}\right)\\
=&\mathcal{O}\left(||{\mathcal{D}}||^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})\cdot 2^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}}\right)\end{array}

Hence, since
Proposition

4.12
.
2
implies
\mathsf{rk}({\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}})\leq|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d})|\in\mathcal{O}(|\mathsf{base}(\mathcal{D}^{d})|)=\mathcal{O}(||{\mathcal{D}^{d}}||_{0})
, by
Lemma

5.16
.
1
-
3
there is some
k>0
depending solely on
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})
and a constant
c>0
such that:

{\llfloor{\mathcal{D}^{d-1}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{{\llfloor{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{e}}^{d}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}{\llfloor{\mathcal{D}^{d}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}}}

for any
0\leq d\leq\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1
.
This means that
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\llfloor{\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}
. But

\llfloor{\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}=\mathcal{D}^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D})+1}
by
Theorem

5.18
. Therefore, we have that
\llfloor{\mathcal{D}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}}||^{k}}
rewrites by the shallow cut elimination strategy to a cut-free
\mathsf{hyp}
-free derivation.

From the polynomial modulus of continuity on cut elimination we obtain our soundness theorems for
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
.

Theorem 5.27

(Soundness)
.

Let
f:(\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*})^{n}\to\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
:

(1)

If
f
is representable in
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
then
f\in\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
;

(2)

If
f
is representable in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
then
f\in\mathbf{FP}
.

Proof 5.28
.

We only show the case where
f
is unary for the sake of simplicity. Let
\underline{f}\in\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
represent
f
, and let us consider the following coderivation, with
s=b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
:

\small\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}&\coloneqq&{{{{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 13.61191pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.37492pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\hskip 8.0pt$}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.24994pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-2.5pt\raise 7.23433pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle\underline{f}}$}}}}}}}}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 13.24994pt\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 13.24994pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}\kern 0.37492pt}\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${\mathbf{S}[]}\multimap\mathbf{S}$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\hskip 8.0pt$}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.24994pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-2.5pt\raise 7.23433pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle\underline{s}}$}}}}}}}}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 13.24994pt\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 13.24994pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.72223pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 7.72223pt}\hbox{\kern 7.72223pt\hbox{\hbox{$\mathbf{S}[]$}}\kern 7.72223pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\multimap}_{e}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to55.27733pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 7.72223pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 24.68037pt\hbox{\hbox{$\mathbf{S}$}}\kern 32.4026pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\end{array}

By
Lemma

5.25
there are
\mathcal{D}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{m}
such that:

\llfloor{\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot||{\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}}||^{k}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{1}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\ldots\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{m}=\underline{f(s)}

for some constant
c>0
, and for some
k>0
depending solely on
\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{f(s)})=\mathbf{d}(\underline{f})
(since
\mathbf{d}(\underline{s})=0
). In particular,
||{\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}}||\in\mathcal{O}(||{\underline{s}}||)=\mathcal{O}(|\underline{s}|)=\mathcal{O}(|s|)
, where
|s|
is the size of the string
s
. So, we have:

\llfloor{\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot|s|^{k}}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{1}\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\ldots\to_{\mathsf{cut}}\mathcal{D}_{m}=\underline{f(s)}

for some constant
c>0
and some
k>0
depending solely on
\mathbf{d}(\underline{f})
. Moreover:

•

By
Proposition

5.21
we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}|\llfloor{\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}}\rrfloor_{c\cdot|s|^{k}}|&\in&\mathcal{O}(|s|^{k\cdot\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{f(s)})+1}\cdot||{\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}}||^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{f(s)})+1})\\
&=&\mathcal{O}(|s|^{k\cdot\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{f})+1}\cdot|s|^{\mathbf{d}(\mathcal{D}_{f})+1})\\
&=&\mathcal{O}(|s|^{k\cdot\mathbf{d}(\underline{f})+1}\cdot|s|^{\mathbf{d}(\underline{f})+1})=\mathcal{O}(|s|^{h})\end{array}

for some
h>0
depending solely on
\mathbf{d}(\underline{f})
.

•

By
Proposition

4.12
, we have
m\in\mathcal{O}(|s|^{3h})
and
|\mathcal{D}_{i}|\in\mathcal{O}(|s|^{h})
.

This means that we can construct a polysize family of circuits
\mathcal{C}=(C_{n})_{n\geq 0}
such that, for any
n\geq 0
, on input
s=b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}^{*}
,
C_{n}(s)
evaluates
\mathcal{D}_{f(s)}
to
\underline{f(s)}
and returns
f(s)
. Therefore,
f\in\mathbf{FP}/\mathsf{poly}
. Suppose now that
f
is representable in
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
. Then
\underline{f}
is regular, and so the function
n\mapsto C_{n}
can be constructed uniformly by a Turing machine. Moreover, it is easy to see that this Turing machine works in polynomial time (actually even in logarithmic space). Therefore,
f\in\mathbf{FP}
.

6.
Completeness

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ax}\;

x:A\vdash x:A

\Gamma,x:\sigma\vdash M:B

\scriptstyle{\multimap}_{i}\;

\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.M:\sigma\multimap B

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\multimap B

\Delta\vdash N:\sigma

\scriptstyle{\multimap}_{e}\;

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash MN:B

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\Delta\vdash N:\tau

\scriptstyle{\otimes}_{i}\;

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M\otimes N:\sigma\otimes\tau

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma\otimes\tau

\Delta,x:\sigma,y:\tau\vdash P:C

\scriptstyle{\otimes}_{e}\;

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{let\ }{x}\otimes{y}={M}\mathsf{\ in\ }{P}:C

\Gamma\vdash M:A

\scriptstyle{\forall}_{i}\;

\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X.A

\Gamma\vdash M:\forall X.A

\scriptstyle{\forall}_{e}\;

\;(\star)

\Gamma\vdash M:A[B/X]

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{I}}_{i}\;

\vdash\mathsf{I}:\mathbf{1}

\Gamma\vdash N:\mathbf{1}

\Delta\vdash M:C

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{I}}_{e}\;

\Gamma,\Delta\vdash\mathsf{let\ }\mathsf{I}=\,{N}\mathsf{\ in\ }{M}:C

\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma

\scriptstyle\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}\;

\oc\Gamma\vdash M:\oc\sigma

\Gamma\vdash M:\tau

\scriptstyle\wn\mathsf{w}\;

\Gamma,x:\oc\sigma\vdash M:\tau

\Gamma,y:\sigma,z:\oc\sigma\vdash M:\tau

\scriptstyle\wn\mathsf{b}\;

\Gamma,x:\oc\sigma\vdash M[x/y,x/z]:\tau

\vdash\mathbf{M}:(0):\sigma

\vdash\mathbf{M}:(1):\sigma

\ldots

\vdash\mathbf{M}:(n):\sigma

\ldots

\scriptstyle\mathsf{stream}\;

\{\mathbf{M}(i)\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is finite

\vdash\mathbf{M}:\omega{\sigma}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{disc}\;

\vdash\mathsf{disc}:\omega{\sigma}\multimap\mathbf{1}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{pop}\;

\vdash\mathsf{pop}:\omega{\sigma}\multimap\sigma\otimes\omega{\sigma}

Figure 17
.
Typing rules for system
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
with
(\star)\coloneqq\mbox{$B$ is $(\oc,\omega)$-free}
.

In this section we establish the completeness theorem for
\mathsf{wr}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
and
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}^{\infty}
(
Theorem

6.38
). To this end we introduce
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
, a type system designed to express computation with access to bits of streams, and we show that the system can encode polynomial time Turing machines with advice. By a similar reasoning, polynomial time computable functions can be represented in
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
, a stream-free subsystem of
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
. We then translate the type systems into
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
, respectively (
Theorem

6.36
), and conclude by the simulation theorem relating the inductive and non-wellfounded proof systems (
Theorem

4.39
).

6.1.
The type systems
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}

The type system
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
is a type-theoretical counterpart of
\mathsf{nuPLL}_{2\ell}
, where the linearity restriction in the second-order rules of
Figure

2
is duly reflected by a weaker polymorphism, and modal formulas “
\omega\sigma
” express types of streams. We also introduce
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
, the stream-free subsystem of
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
corresponding to
\mathsf{PLL}_{2\ell}
.

Definition 6.1
.

[
\Lambda_{\mathsf{stream}}
]
We define
\Lambda_{\mathsf{stream}}
as the set of terms generated by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}M&\coloneqq&x\ |\ \mathsf{I}\ |\ \mathsf{let\ }\mathsf{I}=\,{x}\mathsf{\ in\ }{M}\ |\ M\otimes M\ |\ \mathsf{let\ }{x_{1}}\otimes{x_{2}}={M}\mathsf{\ in\ }{M}\\
&&\lambda x.M\ |\ MM\ |\ \mathbf{M}\ |\ \mathsf{disc}\ |\ \mathsf{pop}\end{array}

where
x
ranges over a countable set of term variables and
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(0)\!::\!\mathbf{M}(1)\!::\!\ldots
is a stream of terms. Meta-level substitution for terms, written
M[N/x]
, is defined in the standard way. The reduction rules for
\Lambda_{\mathsf{stream}}
are the following:

\small\begin{array}[]{ll}(\lambda x.M)N\to_{\beta}M[N/x]&\\
\mathsf{let\ }{x_{1}}\otimes{x_{2}}={M\otimes N}\mathsf{\ in\ }{P}\to_{\beta}P[M/x_{1},N/x_{2}]&\mathsf{let\ }\mathsf{I}=\,{\mathsf{I}}\mathsf{\ in\ }{M}\to_{\beta}M\\
\mathsf{pop}\,\mathbf{M}\to_{\beta}{hd}{(\mathbf{M})}\otimes{tl}{(\mathbf{M})}&\mathsf{disc}\,\mathbf{M}\to_{\beta}\mathsf{I}\end{array}

and apply to any context, where
{hd}{(\mathbf{M})}
and
{tl}{(\mathbf{M})}
are meta operations returning, respectively, head and tail of
\mathbf{M}
. With
\to_{\beta}^{*}
we denote the reflexive and transitive closure of
\to_{\beta}
.

Type assignment systems for the standard lambda calculus that are based on linear logic do not satisfy subject reduction, i.e., preservation of typing under normalisation
[
BT04
]
. Following
[
GR07
]
, there are at least two approaches to recover this property:

•

extend the lambda calculus with explicit constructors and destructors corresponding to the exponential modalities.

•

restrict types to prevent the system typing pathological lambda terms.

Mazza’s type systems for parsimonious logic follow the first approach
[
Maz14
,
Maz15
,
MT15
]
. In this paper we rather adopt the second approach, which will allow us to work with a much simpler system and avoid technicalities. Specifically, we consider the restricted class of
essential types
introduced in
[
GRDR09
]
. Roughly, with essential types the exponential modalities cannot occur to the right of an implication, so that types of the form
A\multimap\oc A
are forbidden. This restriction prevents the system expressing forms of sharing and duplication of data, which cause the failure of subject reduction.

Definition 1

(
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
)
.

The
essential types
are generated by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{\qquad}r@{\;}c@{\;}l}A&\coloneqq&X\ |\ \mathbf{1}\ |\ \sigma\multimap A\ |\ \forall X.A&\sigma&\coloneqq&A\ |\ \sigma\otimes\sigma\ |\ \oc\sigma\ |\ \omega{\sigma}\end{array}

(6)

where
X
ranges over a countable set of type variables. We denote by
\sigma[\tau/X]
the meta-level substitution of
\tau
for the free occurrences of the type variable
X
in
\sigma
. A
context
is a set of the form
x_{1}:\sigma_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:\sigma_{n}
for some
n\geq 0
, where the
x_{i}
’s are pairwise distinct term variables and
\sigma_{i}
are types. Contexts range over
\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma,\ldots
.
We denote by
\oc\Gamma
a context of the form
x_{1}:\oc\sigma_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:\oc\sigma_{n}
.
The type assignment system for
\Lambda_{\mathsf{stream}}
, called
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
, derives
judgements
of the form
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
according to the typing rules in
Figure

17
. The restriction of
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
without the typing rules
\mathsf{stream}
,
\mathsf{disc}
and
\mathsf{pop}
is called
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
. We write
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}}M:\sigma
(resp.
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}}M:\sigma
) when the judgement
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
is derivable in
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
(resp.
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
), omitting the subscript when it is clear from the context. If
\mathcal{D}
is a typing derivation of
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
then we write
\mathcal{D}:\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
.

Essential types ensure subject reduction for
\mathsf{PTA}_{2\ell}
and
\mathsf{nuPTA}_{2\ell}
. To show this, we start with a structural property of the type systems that is straightforward consequence of the linearity restrictions introduced by the essential types.

Proposition 6.2
.

If
\mathcal{D}:\Gamma\vdash M:\oc\sigma
then
\Gamma=\oc\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{D}
is obtained from a typing derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
by one application of
\mathsf{f\mathsf{\oc p}}
, followed by a series of applications of
\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\wn\mathsf{b}
.

Proof 6.3
.

Straightforward, by induction on
\mathcal{D}
.

We now introduce substitution properties for both types and typable terms.

Lemma 6.4
.

If
\Gamma\vdash M:\sigma
then
\Gamma[\vec{C}/\vec{X}]\vdash M:\sigma[\vec{C}/\vec{X}]
for every
\vec{C}=C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
and
\vec{X}=X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.

Lemma 6.5

(Substitution)
.

If
\mathcal{D}_{1}:\Gamma,x:\tau\vdash M:\sigma
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}:\Delta\vdash N:\tau
then there is a typing derivation
S(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})
of
\Gamma,\Delta\vdash M[N/x]:\sigma
.

Proof 6.6
.

The proof is by induction on the lexicographic order over
(h(\mathcal{D}_{1}),s(\tau))
, where
h(\mathcal{D}_{1})
is the height of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
s{(\tau)}
is the number of symbols of the formula
\tau
. The only interesting case is when
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
by applying a
\wn\mathsf{b}
rule, where
M=M^{\prime}[x/y,x/z]
,
\tau=\oc\tau^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
is

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}

\Gamma,y:\tau^{\prime},z:\oc\tau^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma

\scriptstyle\wn\mathsf{b}\;

\Gamma,x:\oc\tau^{\prime}\vdash M^{\prime}[x/y,x/z]:\sigma

By
Proposition

6.2
we have that
\Delta=\oc\Sigma
and

\small\mathcal{D}_{2}\coloneqq{{{}{}{}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 21.00067pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern
